TawxysrsE McDOUGALLS PaMt SELF—MISING FLOIM LI IS THE .13‘E'S't• The Examiner, Saturday, November 19,1904.

"What doesaxan licouten: mean Mother?' 431 means the eama, my dear"

opportunity to re-name this particular fabric, as manu- facturers have done in numberless other cases. We are always coming across materials we knew well in the "long ago," bearing high-sounding titles we have never heard before, but velveteen remains velveteen so far as the name goes. But instead of the old-time fabric, with dusty surface and cotton back, which always creased and crumpled upon the smallest provocation, and looked worn out after the first time of wearing, we have a soft, silky, lustrous, uncrushable material, light in weight, and in the most beautiful colours imaginable. For the modish picture gowns, these new velveteens are invalu- able, falling as they do into the most graceful folds, and draperies, almost unaided. - Leather-trimmed Walking Costumes.—All the best walking skirts are built to clear the ground well, and bound at the hem with soft fine leather, the cuffs, collar, and revers of the coat, also the waistbelt being of leather too. For golf, heavier varieties of leather are used, including the expensive, but wear resisting crocodile. The new, complete soft leather blouses, in pale pastel shades, are enjoying a considerable vogue. for donning under motor coats, but whether they are really healthy wear, is a somewhat vexed question. A Sunday thristmas.—Those people who are inclined to regard the festive season as something of a bore,will not be sorry that Christmas falls this yezr on a Sunday. It has been suggested that the preceding Saturday should be made a Bank Holiday, but that would be a most inconvenient arrangement, and if there is to be any extension of the holidays it should be the Tuesday, which will no doubt be generally observed in any case as an off-day. To make Saturday a closed day would be disastrous for the shopkeepers, besides making a general muddle of the Christmas festivities. It would be a wasted kind of day, neither one thing or the other, and there is absolutely nothing to be said in favour of such a proposal. The week-end is wanted for the Christmas shopping, and regarded altogether, a Sunday Christmas makes for the general convenience. Mincemeat for Christmas.—Those housewives who have nor :vet made their mincemeat, should do so with- out delay. Take half a pound each of stoned and chopped raisins, apples (peeled, cored and chopped), sultanas, currnts, mixed peel (well chopped) and al- monds (blanched and chopped). Mix, and add one pound of well chopped beef suet, one and a quarter pounds of brown sugar,- a dessert spoonful of mixed spice, and half a tumbler-full of brandy. Stir thorough- ly and place in jars which should be tied down with parchment paper immediately.

WOhIEN'S CHAT.

Royal Visiting.—The King and Queen have several important engagements to fulfil before returning to London for the Christmas shopping, a business to which the Queen always gives her own personal attention. The past week has been a very busy one at Windsor, in con- sequence of the visit of the King and Queen of Portu- gal, who will prolong their stay in this country over the end of the month, and will meanwhile pay visits to the Duke and Duchesse d' Orleans at Wood Norton, to the Duke and Duchess of Devonshire at Chatsworth and to the Duke and Duchess of Portland at Welbeck Abbey. After the departure gf the King and Queen of Portugal from Windsor, their Majesties, King Edward and Queen Alexandra are going on a short visit to Mr. and Mrs. Willie James, at West Dean Park. The King has stayed at West Dean on several occasions, and the Queen with whom Mrs. James is an especial friend, has also visitedlthere as Princess of Wales. Queen Amelie.—The Queen of Portugal is not so tall and fair as her sister the Duchess of Aosta, and the very tall and heavy figure of King Carlos makes her look shorter than shels. She is nevertheless very handsome and queenly-looking, and possesses all the gifts of the Frenchwoman for a perfect taste in dress. Queen Annslie is much the same age as our own Princesses, being some two or three months the senior of the Prince of Wales, and is still very young-looking. Her love of outdoor life has no doubt helped her to retain her youthful appea:ance, and she is a thorough sportswoman a taste she acquired during her early training in thig country, where her girlhood was spent. The Portugese Ambassador.—The Marquis de Soveral has more than usually had his hands full of engagements in consequence of the visit of the King and Queen of Portugal. He is tall and finely built, and, always a striking figure in the ball-room,he enjoyel formerly the reputation of being the best waltzer in Europe. Then, besides his fine presence, he is the soul of geniality and good fellowship, which make him a welcome guest, wherever he goes. The marquis is therefore always in demand, and he enjoys an unrivalled popularity at Court and in Society. He is cote of the most intimate friends of King Edward, and his name is very seldom absent from the list of guests at any country house where his Majesty may be visiting. Thoughtful Hostesses.—Most women have been through the trying ordeal of dining at a house where the lights have been so arranged as to show up every blemish of age, and place even the most beautiful girl at a disadvantage, and few, however charming the host and hostess may be, care to repeat the unpleasant experience, Hostesses who would be popular, should therefore attend personally to the lighting of their dinner tables, and drawing rooms, and see that this is carried out in the most becoming manner possible. Candles are unquestionably to be preferred, but electric light properly shaded, is almost as good, provided such shades take either a pink or rose hue. Yellow and red rank next, but the latter must not be very deep, or it will keep the light back too touch. Green and blue are colours to be avoided, when electric, Candle, or lamp shades are concerned. Turquoises in Favour.—The craze for turquoises con- tinues, and the jewellers—looking ahead—are 'preparing large quantities of turquoise ornaments, for Christmas gifts. Her Majesty the Queen, the Princess of Wales, and several members of the Royal Family are very fond of this particular gem and many of their wedding gifts take the form of turquoisejevvellery. Perhaps the most beautiful array of turquoises, is owned by Lady Sophie Scott, the younger daughter of Lord and Lady Cadaemn, ornaments containing these blue stones being presented to her by the King and Queen, and several members of the Royal Family, on the occasion of her marriage. Many of the bride's relatives end friends too, knowing of her fancy, sent turquoise jewellery. and all this added to a collection commenced in childhood, makes a magnifi- cent show. Lady Juliet Duff, Lady de Grey's young daughter. also possesses some magnificent turquoises, so does the Dowager Lady Wherncliffe, Mrs. George Cavendish Bentinck, and Mrs. Arthur Bouchier: A Gem for every Month.—The superstition that each month of the year is under the influence of some precious stone, has been gaining ground of late years, and it is now the custom to present—on the occasion of a birthday anniversary—the " correct " gem, which is supposed to bring joy and good luck to its wearer. The stone for January is the garnet, for February the amethyst, for March the blood-stone, for April the diamond, for May the emerald,for June the agate,while for July the cornelian is set apart. For August we have the sardonyx, for September the chrysolite, for October the opal, far November the topaz, while for December the turquoise falls to our lot, promising pros- petity in all the circumstances of life. A CRISIS.

BY LADY GILBERT, Author of "My Silver Jubilee," "A Fellow's Sister," "A First Engagement," "A Sequel," &a.

Nurse Ryder had returned home after long attendance on a hopeless case. She was tired and depressM, and sat at her lonely fire leaning her head on her hand. It was a pale, delicately- featured face, but full of strength and tharacter. The fair hair, uncrowned at the moment by the inevitable nurse's cap, was ruffled in light rings about her brows, her feet were thrust into slippers; she was in her short hour of relaxation from pro- fessional work. As she stared into the red coals, mechanically turning a wife's gold ring on her finger, a loud knock resounded on the hall-door below, and a sound of voices rose from the hall. In another moment the door of her apartment was opened, and a dostor whom she knew entered in haste. "Come," he said, "not an instant to lose. A bad case of fever. The nurse has broken down, and there will probably be a crisis to-night--" "But I have just got back from --shire—a long journey—and I feel unfit. My patient died--" "He wouldn't have died in your hands if anyone could have saved him. Conic."

Nurse Ryder picked up her bonnet and put it on. Her luggage, not yet unpacked, was seized and carried off, and a few minutes later she was driving through London with the doctor, in his lvougliam. She gazed wearily through the splashing rain at the blurred lights of the 'ow lamps, her ears intern- -gently open to the information and instructions which the doctor was giving her, but part of her brain still coloured by a remnant of the thoughts which the doctor's summons had interrupted. One street, one house in London was much like another. She did not care where she was going, only knew that she was wanted in much baste to save a.life. "This is the house," said the doctor, as the brougham pulled tip; and he sprang out, giving Nurse Ryder his hand. She followed him up the steps and into a hall. Had she not been in this bail before? London hOuses are so much alike. But surely that—that---- Her eyes went from one object to another. _ _

"Conle along," said the doctor, and hurried up the lighted staircase. She followed him, now thoroughly on the alert. Shewn into a room beside the sick-room, she paused a moment before throwing off her cloak, and looked around with a long look of wonder and anguish. . "It 13 true," she miirmured ; "it is the house. And everything undisturbed. I must not dare stop to think ! Yes, doctor, lam ready." And Nurse Ryder slipped noiselessly into the sick-room after the doctor, who gave one satisfied glance at the expression of serious calm on her countenance.

"It will be a hard fight, but you are the woman for it. The finest fellow in the world—a friend of mine—has a sad story " _

The directions for the night were given, and the doctor departed. Nurse Ryder 'moved about the dimly-lighted room putting everything in order without sound. The patient lay tossing in delirium. She had not as yet seen his face. At last she .stood by the curtain and looked at him through a slight parting in its 'folds, closed them again, and sank on her knees with her face to the wall. No sigh, no sob, no tear. Only a deathly silence as a soul felt itself tmexpectedly confronted with the judgment of God. That was past. The•nurse sat motionless by the shaded lamp the long night through, merely/moving to do what was necessary for the patient. Nurse Ryder was familiar with thrt ravings of fever, and this patient was raving wildly. She never allowed herself to be disturbed, for a good nurse needs all her self-possession, and her mind must be occupied with questions of temperature and the carrying out of the doctor's instructions. Yet with one side of her vigilant brain Nurse Ryder was listening to what this feverish man was saying as though her own life as well as his had been hanging in the balance against his words. "Anna! Anna!" he was muttering, "where have you hidden yourself? I have been up and down all over the world. I've been in-Heaven looking for you. and in Hell too, and still no trace, no trace. Why did you leave me? No, not that.. They said so. I_Was cross and hard with her, but I never asked her a question. You think she would have answered a straight question ? Aye, so do I. But the devil was in me, and I quarrelled with her. She was proud, and she had a right to be. So was I. Neither of us would give way. When she said she would go, I said—what did I say? I can't remember. Something is wrong with my head. I said she might go if she liked, that I was better without her. But. I never thought she would go " 11 He was silent, and presently said, weakly: "Nurse, a drink. What have I been talking about?" ' "Fi el d-daisies and running . streams," said Nurse Ryder, softly, "and little lambs in the meadows. Very sweet things. Try to sleep."

He had fallen back, and relapsed into his fancies. "Who said field-daisies ? It must have been Anna. I told her she was downright silly obout them. She stuck them all over my hair. That was when we were honeymooning in the meadows. I tell you, I nevet thought she would go. But she went; when I came back, she was gone. I shut my- Own house•then, and went too. I've Spent a lot of years travelling, but it doesn't do any good. I tried to get home, but no ship would carry me. They all said to 'each other: He Murdered her.' I looked in the graveyards, but I couldn't see her name on any of them-"

"No, I didn't•murder her ! You shan't say it ! " Ile sprang up in the bed, and looked round with frenzied eyes. At the sound of his voice speaking loudly, a than who was in waiting in an inner room looked in to see if he was wanted, and. crept. to Nurse Ryder's side. But the patient's head had fallen on the pillow again, and he was muttering incoherently. -. "It's all about herself," whispered the faithful servant, an Irishman. "The wife he had! Such a husband to go and leave, and him worshippin' the ground she stepped on! May God forgive her Wherever she is this day ! Gone on the stage she was, somebody told me. If be rages up that way again, miss, I'll be back with ye in half7a-minute. It wouldn't do. to let him sthrive to be gettin' up on us." He crept away again, but before he bad reached the inner room he turned and came noiselessly back again. "Maybe you Would be whisliperin' to him and pretendin' it was herself come back," he said, low, in Nurse Ryder's ear. "It might have an effect .on him. He was terrible fond of her ; and sure it would do no harm to make him think a lie, if it only would soothe his poor brains a bit !" Ile drew back a little, and gave a keen glance at Nurse Ryder's half-averted face. She turned her head a little more fully into the shadow of the curtain, and made a sign for him to go ; and the Irishman went away again, and this I hue disappeared into the other mem. The patient was quiet for a time, and the nurse sat inotionless, her brain ablaze with thoughts that searched as with torches Many a chamber of memory habitually kept shrouded and locked. The locks were broken, Windows thrown open, shrouds were riven, and all that had been hid away stood bare in the light. She saw through the windows lovely halcyon days, a river flowing, the hang grass if fields with their floating wild flowers, all blown one way by the wind. She felt the touch of a strong warm hand holding her own, bird notes and sweet words came to her together. "So liappy so happy, so unutterably happy," was the refrain of all the voices in her 'ears, echoes of the delight, in her heart. Who so good, so great, SO masculine and sweet as the ma.n who had wooed her and won her. Looking through another window further on she saw, shrinking, a vain, silly creature, whose head was turned by flatteries, with mocking words ,for the husband she adored, and with smiles to give to the, unworthy whose presence was a wrong to hip,. Oh, detestable self- love and pri,le I Bane of the woman who, while worshipping a true love can gratify vanity at the cost of misery to the one beloved. Oh ! wheels of tine, have ye not crushed pride into the dust ? Poor little vanity that, after all, died of the effort to live for itself ! Will nothing atone ? 11Iust Death step in and forbid the righting of all Hint' was so wrong ? Had God led her here to his side only for her punishment, that she might look al, and see him die ?

...... _ "Oh, wv husband ! my husband !" groaned the woman in the depths of her heart while no sound 3tirred her

.lEe lad hen quiet for some time, but now .the leliriunt rose to its height again. He. got up tnd tried to Push past her. "Anna! Anna ! " he cried. "Must not I go 41 save her life?"

She sang the tune without the words, the very melody of love, just softly breathed, notes that were whispers of singing :

"But while I've thee before me, With heart so warm and eyes so bright, No cloud can linger o'er me, Thy smile turns them all to light." • The words seemed to be in the music though the melody was only crooned. "Ah, yes," he murmured, "that is Anna! Why did you stay away so long?" "Sleep now," she whispered. "I will sing again if you will sleep." He lay listening. For some time she could see that his eyes were wide open and bright, staring at the wall. She went on with her whispers of song till she saw the eyelids begin to droop. A few notes and she ceased, and the eyes quivered and opened, and again the song went on. The hushing voice wavered away again, the eyelids trembled a little but did not open. A few minutes more and the patient was asleep. Even in sleep he had not relaxed hold of the hand. The nurse sat immovable, afraid even to breathe lest the clasped hand should stir ever so little, and disturb that life-giving slumber. The faithful watcher in the next room crept to the door to satisfy himself of the meaning of the low', unbroken silence, gave one glance at the sick-bed and retired. The hours of the night moved slowly on. Distant tolling of clock-bells checked them off ; so much time over and done with. How many hours of peaceful sleep would mean life, to the fevered maw? .The nurse knew to a minute.

What silent prayers did not the woman say in her heart during the watches of that night. Seven hours more, 0 God. Four hours. Two hours. It was morning, and the sounds of life were coming back in the street. Would they startle and wake him ? "Give him to me, 0 God, give him to me, and the souls of two will thank Thee and obey Thee for all eternity." How many a cry like this has gone up from a bedside, and, in the incomprehensible ordinances of the Creator—Life-giver; Death-giver—has been denied! Nurse Ryder knew it. Were his Maker to take him now it would be in order to give him happiness apart from her who had tortured him. She, too, had been tortured; but let all blame, all punishment be now upon her only. Since she had hidden her remorse and her weariness of life under a nurse's dress, strivinr, to do good to others sirice she could no more for 'him, had she not suffered? Enough to expiate in his eyes faults even worse than all her failings and blunders. And yet, perhaps, not enough in the eyes of Heaven. What was she to mete out her own punishment? And again the cry went up from her heart: "0 God, Thy mercy is greater than Thy justice! Give me a future even on earth in which to atone, to my husband, giving him joy for pain and tenderness for cruelty !"

Silence still, and sleep. Straw had been strewn on the street outside, and steps and wheels went past under the windows without noise. How many hours and Nurse Ryder had not moved.. Her hand wil.s still motionless in the clasp of patient's fingers. _lt was far, On in the day when the sick man opened his eyes and looked around him.: "I have been .ill. You are a nurse," with a glance at the white-capped figure in the shade of the curtain. Her hand had been set free as he began to awake. Then, after a moment's quiet he added: "I fancied it was my wife who was here."

"She has been here,'" said the nurse, softly. "I thought so. Where is she now? I should think you might be she only Anna would not be likely to come and 'nurse me."

"O love, do not say so!" "Anna!" He turned towards her. She had thrown off the nurse's cap and drawn back the curtain. "Great leavens ! have you come back to me?" "Back to you for ever, my darling, if you will have me and forgive me. But rest now and allow me to make you well."

He raised himself to look her in the face, an,cl she Wrapped in her arms and pillowed his head on her skoulder. The door opened softly and the doctor appeared, but paused in the . doorway in amazement at what he beheld.

-Nurse Ryder gave him a smile radiant through tears.

"Come in, doctor." she said: outstretching her disengaged hand. "The crisis is past. You aro the angel who brought me to cure this paticiu, We are husband and .wife."

MAYOR'S SUNDAY.

lit OFFICIAL VISIT TO THE WARRINGTON PARISH CHURCH. ev. Morley Stevenson and Municipal

Problems.

As a fitting way of inaugurating the municipal year, the Mayor (Alderman Boltcn) and members of the Corporation, together with the borough magistrates and other townsmen followed the well-established c,ustom of attending service at the Parish Church on Srunday morning. The weather was ideal for the time the year, and there was a good muster for the pro- cession which left the Town Hall at ten o'clock,headed by a posse of police under the direction of the Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot). As in previous years the members of the local Volunteer Battalion figured prominently, Colonel Edelsten being in command. Fifteen officers and about 200 men paraded, and the excellent band of the battalion played lively selections. The St. John Ambulance corps, in charge Of Captain Waring, and the Fire Brigade, with the superintendent (Mr. F. Turner), were strongly repre- sented. The Mayor, who ,was accompanied by the deputy-mayor (Alderman Fairclough) and Alderman Francomb was preceded by the mace and sword bearers. Members and officials of the Corporation followed, amongst those in attendance being : Aldermen Smethurst, Hutchinson, and Bennett, Coun- cilors Tinnion, Burton' Skelland,Fulton-Smith,Evans, Barton, Sankey, Craik. Wilson, Hulse, Roberts, Stott, Duncan, Benson, Forshaw, Clegg, Ellison, Ashcroft, Wilkinson, P. Peacock, and Brough, Messrs. Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), T. Longdin (borough surveyor), A. M. Ker (assistant surveyor), J. Moore Murray (director of education), E. A. Robson (headmaster of the Technical Institute),C. Madeley (librarian), C. B. Ayl- ward (headmaster of the School of Art), G. S. Pendle- bury (art master), W. H. Woodcock (assistant over-. seer), F. G. Ruddock (public analyist , F Taylor, (secretary of the gas department) W. Flood (inspector of nuisances), and R. Young (cemetery registrar . Other tcwnsmen taking part in the procession were Messrs. R. T. Fairclough, S. Wright, H. 'Thornton, Edwin Woods, W. Richardson, C. S. Burgess, G. Pilling, (justices of the peace), G F. F. White (magistrates' clerk),W.Bibby,G. Thomp- son, R. B. Pearson, J. F. Awbery, A. Wright, H. Ferguson, E. Aukland, G. Wallington, G. Watkins, J. Payton, J.. Hands, S. N. Green, H. Isherwood, W. Rogers, F. Aukland, J. Lee, R. Shacklady, J. Holden, B. Bolton, A. Jolley and others. Numerous ipectators lined the route to the church. Within the Parish Church a very large congregation had assembled. The proceisionists, for whom seats had been reserved in the nave, were met at the entrance by the clergy and choir who preceded them up the centre aisle. The clergy taking part in the service were the rn Rector (Rev. Canon WHIM, the Revs. Morley Steven- son, E. J. Wilcoeks, A. S. Cameron, G. W. Dickinson, and T. A. Ridpath. The choir must be complimented upon their efficient and expressive renderings of the musical parts, the anthem" God, Thou art great" being given in a particularly creditable manner. Mr.F.M.Darby, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ with his accustomed skill. The preacher was the Rev. Morley Stevenson, who based his discourse on the words "Babylon the great," Revelations xvm. 2 ; "The Holy City, new Jerusalem" Revelations xxr. 2. He said they would all feel that the occasion which had brought together that great congregation was one of those annual occasions which had for every inhabitant of this town a very deep and real significance. Roughly speaking, a thousand years ago on the place where the Training College now stood —still called by many inhabitants. The Mount—was the old Moat Court, and in that Court sat side by side the chief magistrate of .the borough and the chief I minister of the church, underneath the shadow of the I ancient Parish church. There Church and State—or Church and town it they would—administered the affairs of justice and legislated for the welfare of the city. During those thousand years in some form or other the town and the Church had gone hand in hand on these occasions until, as they came together that day on that hallowed spot where for centuries the Parish Church of the town had stood, they went back again to the good old custom and welcomed the chief magistrate of the borough and those who aided him in his govern- ment. They rejoiced to feel that he and his colleagues , should come together in that hallowed House of God and begin the year of their civic office by acknowledg- ing Him who was the King of Kings, and asking that divine blessing upon their labours which alone could grant to them success. To the preacher who by the courtesy of the Rector (the Mayor's chaplain) had been invited to speak to them it I seemed as if the subject of all other subjects which I must ever be suggested in some form or other was that of the town, the melfare of the borough in hich their homes, their families, their lives and happiness were placed. The importance of our cities was deepening and increasing every year, for he was sure it was obvi- ous to them all that, for good or for evil, the people of this country were gravitating towards city life. They all loved the beauty 'of nature' and delighted to leave their busy work and to escape from the town with its pressure, rush and haste into the beauties of that lovely country which God had given to man for his rest and refreshment. But the earnest, strenuous, eager life of tbe nation was not in the peaceful I country but in the bustling town, and more and more I people were leaving the country to become dwellers in Ithose great cities, the size and the magnitude of the I population of which constituted in itself one of the most perplexing problems of the day. That fact was bringing to the front certain problems which all must face who cared at all for the welfare of this great nation. Their thoughts had been occupied lately with the whole subject of physical degeneration. What was going to be the effect of city life upon the generation of the future? Were they going to grow up a stunted, anemic, bloodless people. That was a preblem which must be faced, and men were beginning to speak of garden cities, open spaces, and means for promoting the public health. Then there were iutellectual prob- lems. They knew how by the passing of the Education Act the education of the country had been placed to a very large extent with the great authorities I of each locality, and they had to see that every child in I this town was educated and brought up so that be might Ibe fitted to fulfil his part in the life which lay before him. There were also moral problems. They were becoming awake and alive to the dangers of impurity, intemperance, insanitary places and many other matters ' of that kind and they had to face those difficult problems • and see how our cities could be made wholesome, pure, clean and life-giving. Alluding to spiritual problems' the preacher said it had lately been brought before them in a very painful way that attendance at church and other places of worship was by no means what it ought to be. Here the Church had a great problem to face and solve. The preacher proceeded to picture the two cities of infinitely different character which formed the subject of .the text. Would it not be well, he asked for them in these days to consider whether there might not be in our modern cities the seeds of the disease which led to the fall of Babylon, whether our modern life was not becoming very selfish, whether people were not now much set upon wealth, pleasure, luxury and self indulgence, or whether there was still among them that old spirit of unselfishly living not for the pleasure of self, but for the. good of one's fellowmen. They might say that they could not have a city like the new Jersualem, because it was not earth but heaven. That was true; but they could have an ideal—an ideal to avoid and an ideal to imitate. They could ever be saying to themselves "Our city must be as unlike Babylon as possible, and it must be as like the New Jerusalem as we can possibly make it." The more it resembled that heavenly city, the happier, the better, and the holier it would be. I.et each one present that day realise his citizenship and what it meant. They must not think it was only the rulers of the State who had everything to do. Let each citizen resolve to make his house pure, beautiful, bright, happy and good. When they had made their own homes what they ought to be, then let them go forth to the homes of others who were less fortunate than themselves, and help to brighten them. Let them try to bring happiness Nhere there was sorrow' peace where there was enmity, and purity where there was impurity. Let them try to do what they could to brighten, cheer, and improve the lot of their fellow-citizens, who were fellow-citizens even though they might not have the power and responsibility which those in better positions possessed. 'To you Mr. Mayor" con- chided the preacher, "let me say that the church of Christ welcomes you and your colleagues within these walls to-day. We look to you as you enter upon the second year of your office to make all that is done in the government of this great borough of such a char- acter that 'it will lead us on step by step to the realiza- tion of some such ideal as I have tried all too feebly to describe. We are not ignorant of the problems you have to encounter, and we therefore join with you in earnest prayer and supplication to the Most High that His blessing may rest upon you and those who work with you, so that with that divine aid you may be guided, strengthened and encouraged in the great work and in the honourable office which has beeu committed to your charge." The offertory was on behalf of the choir fund. The return of the procession to the Town Hall was witnessed by a large concourse of townspeople.

THE CHESHIRE HUNTING FIELD.

Fox Swims a Mere Twice in a Forty-Five Minutes' Run.

An exceedingly pretty hunt was associated with the South Cheshire meet at Marley Green on Monday. Hounds got away quite sensationally from Marley Moss with a good, wild fox that allowed no obstacle to bar his progress. At lirankelow Moss he plunged into the mere almost opposite Combermere Abbey. Hounds, coming up shortly afterwards, flung themselves into the water, and the field saw at the same time hounds an I fox swim the mere, which hereabouts is half a mile across, and this bold feat was repeated later, the fox swimming the mere below the big wood preparatory to settling to run hard in the open. At Sound Heath he was viewed holding out signals of distress, but scent gradually died away and he escaped. The run lasted forty-live minutes, and hounds hunted finely throughout. ACCIDENTS WITH THE NORTH CHESHIRES. Two of the accidents with the North Cheshire Hounds on Monday were serious. Mr. Gardener, a Liverpool gentleman,fractured several ribs after falling at a fence, and one of the Duke of Westminster's second horsemen broke his collar bone and his leg in attempt- ing to jump a ditch.

A BOILER EXPLOSION.

John Catterall. seventy-one years of age, residing ut Seaview Public-house. Varnworth, Widnes,was admitted to the Accident Hospital, 'Widnes, on Thursday suffer- ing from the effects of a boiler explosion on his shop premises. The force of the explosion brought down the roof, and he was buried in the debris. His condition is very serious.

PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT'S TRIUMPH.

The result of the PreFidential election rin the United States was an even greater tri- umph for Mr. Roosevelt than at first reported, and his victory is the most popular since the days of Lincoln. The electoral votes divided amongst the various states total 476, and before the 'polling it was estimated that 224 were certainties for Roosevelt and 151 for Parker, with tot votes doubtful. But not only did the former poll every one of the doubtful votes, but he even annexed eighteen of his opponent's certainties, with the result that he was pronounced President by the enormous majority of 210! Great rejoicings took place all over America last week to celebrate the result, and the Demo- crats are overwhelmed by the magnitude of their defeat. Judge Parker is said to be a ruined man financially and will be forced to work for his living, and Mr. Bryan and -Mr. Hearst have announced their intention of reorganising the party. At the present moment the Republicans hold full sway *throughout America, and it is all due to the personality of one man—President Roose- velt himself. It was an election of person- alities, as, now tha-t. the Democrats are solid for sound money, there was very little real difference between the electoral planks of the candidates, but Roosevelt from beginning to end dominated Parker, as he has dominated America since the murder of President McKinley. During the past three years he has proved his indomitable pluck and strength of personality on every hand. He has dictated to the Republican Caucus instead of kow-towing; he has bearded the Trusts in their den; he has had a coloured man to dine at the White House; he has made his appointments regardless of North and South prejudice; and in a hundred and one ways he has shown himself a ruler and not a political puppet. He has mastered the Yankees and beaten them on their own ground, and they like it! This, he has announced, will be his last term of presi- dency, but he has already inaugurated a stroll.- line of policy, which will hand his name .'down to history. First, he has invited delegates from the powers to meet at Washington in a new Peace Conference to further the gootl work begun at the Hague. Next, he has opened up negotiations with Great Britain with regard _

to an

arbitration treaty, as Lord Lansdowne an- nounced at the Guildhall. And, working for his ideal of universal eace, he has expressed himself in readiness to act either singly Or in concert with any of the other powers in bringing about peace between Russia .and Japan.

THE HORSHAM ELECTION.

The return of Lord Tumour for the Hor- sham Division of Sussex was not altogether unexpected, as the home counties' seats are usually Conservative and the last majority in this, instance was a large one, but the Liberals had the satisfaction of halving it. The contest was a prolonged and

dilficult one, as

the constituency

is about five hundred miles

square and thinly ,popu-

fated, and most of the election- eering had to be

done by motor,

as there are

practically no railwayfacilities. Moreover, there was unusual keenness dis-

played on both

Sides, as the seat

THE NEW M.P.

was not. con-

tested at

last two elections, and a distinct change o} party feeling has arisen since then. Lord Tumour's chief plank was the Chamberlain programme, whilst Mr. Lestocq Erskine, his opponent, put Free Trade and Chinese Labour in the forefront of his ticket, and on both sides there were plenty of helpers. The Midhurst corner of the constituency is identified with the name of Cobden, and this fact made the fight upon the question of tariff more keen. Cobden's daughter, Mrs. Fisher Unwin, was one of the most active can- vassers on the Liberal side. But the result was a victory for him by 4,388 votes to Mr. Erskine's 3,604, both sides polling more votes than in 1895 when Mr. Heywood John- stone's majority was the large one of 1,484. Lord Tumour thus becomes the • youngest member of Parliament, being only just of age and still at Oxford. He is, however, well known throughout the constituency and an officer in the Sussex Imperial Yeomanry.

THE NORTH SEA INQUIRY.

Negotiations between Great Britain and Russia have progressed satisfactorily so far that the scope and details of the International Inquiry have now been finally arranged, although up to the time of writing the names of the court have not been divulged, if settled. This happy result is largely di, to the work of Sir Charles Hardinge and Count Benckendorff at the respective clpitals, aided by the splendid diplomacy of M. Paul Cambon, the French kmbassador in London,

M. PAUL CAMBON.

SIR CHARLES HARDINGE. COUNT BENCKENDORFF, who has laboured unceasingly for peace. The terms of the Convention are as follows: First, the Commission is to consist of five members, namely, admirals of Great Britain, Russia, France, and the United States, with a fifth Commissioner to be selected by agree- ment between them. If they cannot agree the choice is to be entrusted to the King of a country subsequently to be determined upon, most probably, it may be added, the King of Denmark. TWO NOTABLE DEATHS.

At the end of last .week occurred the deaths of two very interesting men, who have often been brought before the public notice. These were Mr. Val. Prinsep, R.A., and Mr. G. L. Watson, the famous yacht designer. The former was out and about until within a day or two of his death and succumbed to an internal operation which suddenly became necessary. He was born in India in 1838, and was christened " Val- entine " after the day of his birth, but for many years it has been universally shortened down to the familiar monosyllable. He first exhibited in the Academy in 186 z a picture

MR. G. L. WATSON. MR. VAL. PRINSEP. named "Bianca Capella," after having studied in France. From .the start he was most successful, and attained great popularity with "The Lion's Sons." In 1877 he was selected to paint the official picture of the great Durbar at which the late Queen was proclaimed Empress of India, and in 1880 it was exhibited throughout the country. Meanwhile he was elected an A.R.A. in 1879, and in 1894 he became a .full R.A. In 1900 he was appointed Professor of Painting at the Academy in succession to Professor Herkomer. Mr. Val. Prinsep was a fine, big man, and married a daughter of Mr. Leyland, of the great shipping line, who commissioned Whistler to paint the now famous Peacock Room in his house. Mr. G. L. Watson, on the other hand, has been in bad health for some little time, so much so that recently he refused a commission from Sir Thomas Lipton -to build a fourth "Shamrock." Born only fifty-three years ago, he quickly came to the front as a yacht-designer after building the " Vril," which won many races on the Clyde for Mr. Coates, the great cotton manufacturer. In 1880 he buiit a ninety- ton yacht, the " Vanduara," ,which beat the then Prince of Wales's crack yacht, the "Formosa," time after time. After that big successes followed one on top of the other till in 1887 he built his first challenger for the America Cup—the Thistle. Next he built the famous " Britannia " for the King, and more recently he designed Lord Dunraven's three " Valkyries " and two of Sir Thomas Lipton's "Shamrocks." In all, Mr. G. L. Watson designed over four hun- dred vessels, and at the time of his death his office was full of uncompleted com- missions.

OUR LATEST LITTLE WAR.

One of the inevitable results and burdens of a great Empire is the fact that by force of circumstances we are practically never without some war or other, varying in magnitude and importance, within his Majesty's dominions. These wars are usually quite small affairs, the consequence of the hostility of some native tribe on the outskirts of the Empire and the necessity for subduing and making an example of the chief, to en- courage the others. The latest of these little wars is an expedition against the Nyam-Nyam tribe in the Bahr-el-Ghazal district of the Soudan, three hundred miles from Fashoda. The Nyam-Nyam country is a rich tract of land, containing coal, iron ore, rock salt, and saltpetre, and it has

===== 0002601_19041119_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
MAP OF NYAM-NYAM.

recently been attempted to develop it. The Nyam-Nyams, however, have been bought over by some, Belgian traders, who have sold them a good supply of Remington rifles, and while they forward Belgian interests they have actually gone so far as to fire on our troops. Last February a British patrol under Colonel Wood Was escorting a caravan through the country, and without warning it was attacked and Major Heymer and two Egyptian soldiers were Further, the Nvam-Nyams are calt.._ is, but no opposi- tion or affront was offered to the British till the Belgiaxt traders made their appear- ance. The tribe is about 25,000 Strong, but the majority are only armed with bows and arrows, and it is considered sufficient to send 2. O 0 Men of the Egyptian Army, together -fri!li a maxim battery and a mounted in- fantry corp:;. Sir Reginald Wingate, the Sirdar, is personally organising the expedition which will start shortly as the rainy season i, now over. The journey to the Nyam- Nyam country will occupy from three to five weeks according to the state of the country and the movements of the tribe, who may be anywhere from six to eight hundred miles south of Khartoum. The troops will proceed by steamer to Meshva- er-Rek and then march up the White Nile Valley. A permanent telegraph will be estab- lished as the expedition is expected to result is important developments.

THE NORTHERN UNION.

LANCASHIRE v. YORKSHIRE.

An Easy Win for the White Rose.

Hockenhall's Debut and Injury.

The annual battle of the roses under Northern Union auspices was played on Saturdayat Oldham. It was an ideal football day,but the attendance was moderate for a Lancashire v. Yorkshire Northern Union event. The number of persons who paid for admission was 7,645, and with local members the attendance would be about 8,500. The receipts amounted to £2OB. Teams ; Yorkshire.—Gunn (Bradford), back: Dechan (Brad- ford), Mosby (Bradford), Joe Riley (Halifax), and W P. Davies (Batley), three-quarter backs; Marsden and Surman (Bradford), half backs; Laidlaw (Bradford), Starks (Hull Kingston Rovers), Kilburn (Hull),Osborne (Hull Kingston Rovers), Brook (Hunslet), Langhorn and Morton (Halifax), and H. Wilson (Hunslet), for- wards.

• Lancashire.—Poole (Broughton Rangers), back; Hogg (Broughton Rangers), Taylor (Widnes), Harry (Brough- ton Rangers), and Fish (Warrington), three-quarter backs ; Lomas (Salford) and Hockenhall (Warrington), half backs; Preston (Warrington), Ferguson (Oldham), O'Neil (Leigh),Thomas (Oldham),Prescott (St. Helens), Cheetham (Swinton), Trotter (Broughton Rangers),and Buckler (Salford), forwards. Referee, Mr. J. Bruckshaw (Cheshire). Touch judges Mr. J. B. Cooke (Yorkshire) and Mr. J. Houghton (Lancashire). Winning the toss, lancashire took advantage of the breeze,and Harry putting in a good return to Starks' kick- off, the Red Rose representatives at once pressed. They kept up the pressure for about five minutes during which time Fish thrice repulsed attempts by the York- shire backs to gain relief, his clever fielding and punt- ing beins heartily cheered. Yorkshire at length did get some'relief, but a pass from Harry to Fish enabled the latter to reach the Yorkshire" 25 " line before being _ _

pulled down by Dechan and Gunn. Then Lomas made a mark, but failed to land a goal. Fish once more got, off, but was tackled by Osborne, and sustained tempor- ary injury. He resumed, and again showed up with a strong kick, which compelled Gunn to touch down. The ball went a little later from Surman to Marsden, and then to Riley, whose pass to Mosby was intercepted by Fish. The latter was getting away in a dangerous manner, when Mosby came upon him from behind and brought him down. Yorkshire had the first point scored against them in the shape of a penalty goal kicked by Fish 17 minutes from the start. The score was the outcome of Yorkshire packing four men in the front row of the scrummage. Two minutes later a sensational score placed Yorkshire three more points in arrear. Surman had opened a passing bout, and Riley was continuing the movement at centre when Fish rushed in and knocked the ball down, picked it up again, and dodging Gunn with ease, scored a try under the posts after a really brilliant run. The try-getter missed a very easy goal-shot. After this both teams seemed deter- mined to make play as interesting and open as possible. Yorkshire continued to have many anxious moments. Hogg and Taylor got away with a fine dribble, and near- ing the line the latter kicked across the field. Then ensued a wild rush, which all but ended in a score. Ih fact Hockenhall touched the ball over the line but and it kicked from under him. The apparent try was not allowed and most unfortunately the Warrington half back had to be carried off the field, and thereafter took no part in the game. He was severely cut on the head. Yorkshire looked like scoring just before the interval when, after passing by Surman, Marsden. and Riley, "Wattle " Davies got under weigh. The Batley wing, however, could not pass Poole, who tackled him splen- didly. Then Marsden got away, but was not supported and at half-time the score read :—Lancashire 1 goal 1 try (5 points), Yorkshire nil. The continued absence of Hockenhall caused several changes to take place in the Lancashire back division. raylor went half-back, Ferguson, from the forwards, went full back, while Poole joined Hogg in the three- ' quarter line. Soon after the re-start, Marsdea, when within his own half, initiated a bout of passing. He made a fine opening for Mosby, who in turn passed with good indgment to Davies, and the last-named ran in easily. Mosby failed at goal. Play now became even more interesting than before. Hockenhall's absence was keenly felt by Lancashire, and for a long period their backs had a very anxious time. Two attacks by Davies and Mosby were almost successful, but at length Davies, receiving a pass from M irsden, successfully eluded Hogg and Poole, and scored a try near the left- hand corner-flag. He, also failed• to convert. With a one point lead Yorkshire were with difficulty held in check. Dechan got over after a brilliant round of pass - ing, but the final pass was forward. Yorkshire con- tinued to make splendid use of their advantage in the scrummage, and the backs were given numerous oppor- tunities. From one movement Mosby scored after passing by Marsden and Riley, and thus made the York- shire position tolerably secure, even though Mosby failed with the goal-shot. The same player failed a few minutes later with a penalty kick at goal. The Lanca- shire backs made a spasmodic burst after this, Fish running speedily and tackling Gunn in possession. They were soon repulsed, however, and Marsden, after fine passing and repassing, with Davies; scored a try, which Mosby converted. Result :—Yorkshire 1 goal ana 4 tries (14 points), Lancashire 1 goal and 1 try (5 points)

THE DINNER. ENCOURAGEMENT OF JUNIOR FOOTBALL.

The Northern Union officials, the competing teams. and a number of guests sat down to dinner at the Prince's Restaurant after ehe match at Oldham.

Mr. R. Collinge, Rochdale, presided, and proposed the toast of the winning team. He said he was sure he was voicing the opinions of all their Lancashire friends when he heartily congratulated Yorkshire on their victory. (Applause). He believed that it was for the best interests of the game that victory should not always rest with one county in these matches, and as Lancashire had been very successful for many years they could well afford to lose that day. He was not going to attempt to make excuses or to point out that during the first portion of the game Lancashire were leading, nor would he suggest that it might have been different had not the accident happened to Hockenhall, whom they were all glad to see present. (Applause.) York- shire might still have won. (Applause). He congratu- lated their Yorkshire friends on the evidences that they wished to progress, and that they wanted the game to flourish and prosper. The admirable work that York- shire were doing in connection with schools football and junior football was .an example that every other county might profit by. He knew that the effort which was being made by Yorkshire entailed a vast amount of labour and time and no small amount of money, and he honoured the gentlemen who were taking part in the good work. Mr. F. Lister, Bradford, responding, said Lancashire had won the championship often enough, and he could

No Breakfast Table complete without

assure them that Yorkshire were trying to follow their example. (Cheers.) After thanking Mr..Collinge for his complimentary remarks with regard to Yorkshire's efforts to promote junior and schoolboys' football, he said that that effort was due in a great measure to their Cumberland friends. They had had great support from the Northern Union, and he was sure that the Union did not grudge the money they had given to Yorkshire for that purpose. Mr. J. B. Cooke, proposing the toast of" Lancashire County," said those who had to do with the 4anage- ment of the game should, in legislating for it, lemem- ber that the prosperity of the Union was not to be found in the wealth of h df-a-dozen clubs, but in the ninny clubs. (Applause.) He was perfectly satisfied that there would be no outcry for Association if they had such games as that seen that thy. That was a matter which rested with the players themselves, and he asked why could they not always play a game such as they had played that day. The toast of "The Referee" was also drunk, and responded to by Mr. Bruckshaw.

NORTHERN LEAGUE.

First Division.

WARRINGTON v. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS.

Warrington were in the unenviable plight on Satur- day of having to tackle the Rovers at Hull minus four such players as Fish,Hockenhall,Preston and Boardman, the first three of whom were assisting the County. The Rovers were short of Starks and Osborne (forwards) from a similar cause, but were otherwise strongly repre- sented. Five thousand people witnessed the match which went in favour of the home side from the out- set. Teams:

Hull Kingston Rovers—Sinclair, back; Phipps, Maley, and West, three-quarter backs; Gordon and Chambers, half backs; Kemp, Windle, Speakman, Ellis, Gorman, Townsley, Reed, and Fletcher, forwards. Warrington—Hallam, back; Short, Isherwood, Dickinson, and Harris, three-quarter backs; Davies and Brooks, half backs; Thomas, Shugars, Belton, Cook, Naylor, Jolly. Harmer, and Morrison. forwards. Referee, Mr. J. H. Graham, St. Helens. Touch judges. Messrs. H. Tomes, York, and J. H. Skinner, Halifax.

The Rovers soon obtained the lead, CarmichA kick- ing a fine penalty goal, and Madley, intercepting a pass soon afterwards by slim tactics, scored a try, which was converted by Carmichtel. In the course of a vigorous attack by the Rovers, against which Warrington seemed practically helpless, Chambers scored in the corner, bringing the half-time figures to 10 points to nil. The visitors' defence was firmer and of more avail after the restart, and they seemed likely to turn the tables. But as time wore on both sides slackened, and scrummages were much too frequent to be interesting. A startling turn was reserved for the finish by a brilliant rim on the part of West who received from Chamhers at mid-field, and scored a smart try. The place kick was too difficult for Carmichael. Result :—Hull Kingston Rovers, 2 goals, 3 tries (13 points) ; Warrington nil.

===== 0002601_19041119_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN v. BRADFORD.

This match was played at Runcorn before 2,000 spectators. Bradford had six of their best players taking part in the county match at Oldham, and sub- stitutes also had to be found for Hutt, Smales, and Sherratt. Runcorn missed Richardson and Jolley. Walker started for Runcorn, and after several scrumT mages in the centre, the visitors forced the game to the home half, where Houghton, in endeavouring to return mulled the ball. Eagers smartly got possession and scored for Bradford. Dobson failed at goal. Bradford continued the attack, and Eagers sending the ball to the Runcorn end, a race between Houghton and Dobson ended in the Bradford man grounding the ball. Feather added a goal. Butterworth, Walker, Price, and Moran gained ground with passing, and Jones received a fine pass from Hesketh, but knocked the ball forward when a score seemed certain. Brear and Wilson relieved, but the home team again got a footing in the Bradford half, but at half-time Bradford led by 1 goal 2 tries (8 points), to Runcorn nil. After the interval Feather re-started for Bradford. Butterworth, Hesketh, and Price took play close to the Bradford line, and the ball being sent over,a scrum. mage was ordered, from which Brear relieved. A moment later Ward was forced to kick dead. Resuming Eagers and Connell got past the centre with passing, and Barker, Turner, and G -Pen, further improved Brad- ford's position, the latter being stopped by Butterworth just in time. Rnncorn were severely pressed, but a free kick at length brought relief, but only temporarily, as aline piece of passing by Barker, Brear, and Green- wood, ended in the last-named scoring a try, but Feather failed at goal,Result :—Bradford 1 goal 3 tries (11 points), Runcorn nil. Teams : Runcorn.—S. Houghton, back; Jones. Hesketh, Price. and Moran, three-quarter backs ; Butterworth and Walker, half backs; Moulton, A. Richardson, Higgins, C. Houghton, Tomlinson, Dixon, Padbnry, and Dobson forwards.

SWINTON v. LEIGH.

Played at Swinton before 3,009 spectators. Evans started for Swinton, and a free kick to Leigh caused Swinton to defend but Gartrell brought relief. The visitors were then penalised, but Molyneux failed at goal. After a neat round of classing among nearly the whole of the Swinton backs, Gartrell ran over at the corner, but Flynn failed at goal. Johnson put in a fine run and kick on behalf of Leigh, and Dixon had all his work cut out to save the situation. Subsequently Bennett and Neville were conspicuous, while a little later Messer was pulled down near the home posts. An attack by Swinton followed, and Massey receiving the ball from a doubtful pass forward, ran over the line,and the referee allowed a try. Flynn kicked a goal. Neville scored an unimproved try for Leigh. Result:—Swinton 1 goal and' Iwo tries (8 points), Leigh 1 try (3 points).

HUNSLET v. WIDNES.

At Hunslet. Five minutes after the start W.Goldthorpe opened Hunslet's score with a penalty goal, and though there was some good combined play, and strong runs by W. Walsh, Horsfall, and Jackson, nothing else was scored till just before half-time when Wray scored, W. Goldthorpe kicking a goal from the touch-line. Hunslet's lead at half-time was therefore seven points. 'Gill, Peacock. and Bennett strove very hard to put a better appearance on things for Widnes at the start of the second half, but their scoring attempts were beaten off, and then in quick succession Jackson scored from A. Goldthorpe's pass, and also di opped a goal from the back of the scrummage, and W. Goldthorpe made a fair catch, and placed a goal from the mark. In the last minyte Gill broke clear away, and scored for Widnes, Taylor adding the goal points, though it was generally thought that his kick at goal had failed. Score :—Hunslet 4 goals and 2 tries (14 points), Widnes 1 goal and 1 try (5 points).

At Latchford on Saturday. The home team won the toss and the visitors kicked off. Walker's then got the ball in the home 25, but Ashton returned with a good kick in touch, at the half way line. WONDERS OF THE SKIN.

Nature's Newly-Discoverd Secret.

Of all the wonderful contrivances of Nature, the human skin is perhaps the most wonderful. It is not merely a protective layer, but a heat regulator for our bodies, and an elaborate breathing apparatus, possessing not less than 2,500 miniature "mouths" to the square inch. Through these mouths or pores two pounds of sweat and vapour, which the body can well do without, is expelled daily. In winter these pores grow smaller, proving that the skin is very sensitive to cold. The skin is also very liable to injury and disease, more so perhaps than any other part of the body. Cuts aud bruises. sores and blemishes are very common ; so that the re- cent discovery of juices in certain plants, capable of producing new healthy skin, will have great local interest. The discovery of Zam-bukos the juices in their present refined state are named, was made by an eminent chemist, who, being aware of the dangers and imperfections of existing ointments, salves and embro- cations, determined to work out a novel theory—that inasmuch as Nature has given to man the instinct to rub a place that hurts, so she must have secreted some- where the true healing substance to rub it with. Con- sequently the scientist turned his attention to the herbs and shrubs growing beyond the borders of civilization. _ _ _

At length' after a wearying search, certain rare plants were found from which it was possible to extract juices possessing the astonishing power of creating new healthy skin When applied to a cut or sore, inflamma- tion and disease would be at once taken out—like ink- stains removed from a cloth—and new sound tissue grown completely over the affected place. Here,indeed, was an important discovery! To this unique extract were added other juices and balsams of high medicinal merit; the whole was refined and concentrated so as to increase the potency of each element. and Zam-buk, as we now know the great healer, thus came into existence The secret of its manufacture now belongs to the Zam-buk company. 4, Red-cross-street, London, E.C., who have been able to bring the price of the balm within the reach of all. Zam-buk may be had of all chemists, or post free direct from the proprietors, for one and three half-pencel box, or two and nine for a large family size, containing nearly four times the quantity. Zam-Buk is specially recommended for cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, chatings, itching sores, cramp, stiff joints, raw chins, blackheads, sore throat, sore aching filet, bad circulation, weak ankles, chapped hands, ringworm, colds and chills, sprains, stiffness, blind and bleeding piles, bad legs, swollen knees, inflamed patches, abscesses, boils, ulcers, eczema, running sores, scalp irritation, poisoned wounds, festering sores, dog bites, cat swatches, rheumatism, neuralgia, tic, sciatica, toothache, and for all diseased, injured and irritated conditions of the skin. It is heal- ing, soothing, and antiseptic. Anyone may confidently rub it in, and every home should have a box handy for emergency. It is Nature's true healer, because it pro- duces new healthy skin, and it represents the most valuable secret recently found in the garden of Nature.

THEFT OF FOWLS.

At Earlestown Occasional Court on Monday, Samuel Manes, Earlestown, was charged with having stolen four hens, valued 12s. the property of Thomas William Pritchard, Wargrave-road.—Evidence was given to the effect that on Saturday the fowls were left in a cote secure at 5 p.m„ but at eleven o'clock the same night the four hens were missing. Prisoner was seen by a man named Proctor in the neighbourhood carrying some fowls. When challenged prisoner let the four, hens drop and bolted, but Proctor took up the chase. Prisoner was eventually caught by Proctor and detained until the arrival of the police.—lnspector Innes asked for a remand, which was granted to the 'Warrington Petty Sessions which were held on Wednesday, and at which Maines was sent to gaol for three months with hard labour.

Mr. THOMPSON gavo notice that at the meeting of the Board on December 14th, he would propose the appoint- ment of a small committee to go into the question of the greatly increased cost of vaccination.

THE VALL:A.TION BILL.

After reporting on the annual meetinr, of Poor Law Unions,Mr. JOLLEY said:—OallovemberlOth,l attended the special committee, and on November Ilth, in com- pany with Mr. Brooks and the Clerk, the Conference in London, on the Valuation Bill. The attendance at the Conference was very large, oVer GOO tickets having been issued. The chief business was the consideration of the proposals which had been drafted by the com- mittee, with a view to their being laid before the Government as representing the best way of ensuring real reform, as distinct from more change of method, in respect of valuation. The essential views were in brief the retention of Union areas and committees for assessment purposes; the remodelling of Assessment Committees so that the principal spending authori- ties in each area should be equitably represented upon them, and that the Surveyor of Taxes for the district or other representatives of the Inland Revenue Department should also have a seat; the granting to Assessment Committees enlarged powers, especially in respect of securing accurate information to enable equitable assessment to be made of railways and other special classec of property ; the preservation and extension of existing rights of appeal which were some- what imperilled by the last Valuation Bill, and the recognition of one valuation list for all purposes, imperial and local. The Committee had also specified in their report certain features and proposals of the Government Bill of last session which they deemed objectionable. There was considerable discussion on some of the points, but in the end the whole of the recommendations of the committee were accepted either unanimously or, wnen a vote was taken. by overwhelm- ing majorities. That so large a gathering from all parts of the country, of persons practically acquainted with assessment work should be so unanimous, ought to have weight with the Government, but in view of possible eventualities the committee was asked to remain in office and empowered to act either alone or in conjunc- tion with the Executive of the Poor Law Union Associa- tion.

THE EXAMINER.

NOVICSIBIiR 19th. 1904.

How many of WHAT THE GOVERNMENT our readers, we HAS TO ANSWER FOR. wonder, have read the reports of the spirited and telling indictment of the Government which Sir HENRY CAMPBELL- BANNERMAN, honestest of politicians, delivered at Dundee on Thursday evening? Daily papers do not enter every household, and if they did, the man of the house would not always have tiny) to familiarise himself with their contents. Probably Sir HENRY'S speech has been passed unnoticed by many nay most, of the great army of casual newspaper purchasers. These are the people whose attention we desire to hold at this point. The Liberal leader spoke words of great weight, remarkable significance, and ominous import. He brought right to the front the most heinous of the political crimes which may be laid at the door of the Government whose hour-glass is fast running out. And it is these misdeeds which :must not be allowed to fade from the memory of a single individual qualified to exer- cise the franchise. The "Daily News" admirably emphasizes the points of Sir HENRY'S speech, which was directed largely towards refreshing the public memory with the deplorable facts relating to British South Africa. The journal coMmends the promise, contained in the speech, of a full and honest measure of self- government for South Africa so soon as the Liberals are in power, and adds: "What hope exists of such an act of justice and good faith from a Government whose fortunes are slipping more and more into the hands:of men aiming at the same end as that desired by the Transvaal mine- owners—namely, the use of public powers for private'ends ? As Sir HENRY well said, the cure whichlthe Protectionists offered:for evils which were largely the result of privilege was a further instal- ment of privilege. One estate of the realm, the House of Lords, is founded on privilege. Now the tendency of Conservative politics is to hand over the Home of Commons to a second band of the privileged. We are to make the great financiers and great newspaper mongers' the financial trustees of the British people. What are the funds accumulated by the Tariff Reform League, the immense sums spent on the Tariff Comraission,:the still larger amounts devoted to the purchase of the independent Press, but a vast pool into which men will put their money with the hope of getting it :back with hundreds per cent? We talk of the Trusts in the United States. At this moment one of the greatest, Trusts ever conceived by ambitious wealth is in process of formation on British soil. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is nothing but the Secretary, the Chief Promoter, of that Trust. He may honestly think that he is engaging in a work of statesman- ship. But does he imagine that the manu- facturers who are pouring their contributions into his campaign fund do so for disinterested love of an economic theory? If they do, they are made of different stuff from the tariff-mongers of the Uniten States lt is the first duty of honest statesmanship to tell the country what kind of game is being played with its interests and we are glad to see that Sir HENRY CAMPBELL BANNERMAN has used plain language about a plain issue."

CONFLICTING statements from THE RUSSIAN St. Petersburg have renewed POSITION, the anxiety about the negotia- tions between England and Russia. On one hand, it is affirmed that Russia has not sought to make any substantial modifica- tion in the convention which is to regulate the duties of the International Commission. On the other we are told that the Russian Admiralty has triumphed over the Russian Foreign Office, and that the express agreement described by LordLANs- no wic at the Guildhall in the plainest language is to be repudiated. In:our eyes this would be a

wholly unwarrantable proceeding, but it would be deemed quite natural in Russia. No pledge given by Count LAMSDORFF precludes the Grand Duke ALEXIS from aiming at something dire2tly opposed to it. This is probably why Lord LANSDOWSTE was so explicit at the Guildhall. He wanted to bind Russia in the face of Europe to the obliga- tions which her representative undertook while the Baltic Fleet was within reach of an over- whelming British armament. He took care to have it known that Russia had agreed to let the International Commission fix the blame, if necessary, not only on the four officers admittedly implicated, but pn any others who might be found culpable. This does not suit the Russian Admiralty at all, and we never supposed that it would. But the point is whether Russia will adhere to the only basis on which any inquiry is worth making. It would be idle for the Commission to indicate the responsibility for the tragedy in the North Sea, if it were left to the Grand Duke ALEXIS to deny the right of a foreign tribunal to pass judg- ment on Russian officers. We must hope that Count LAMSDORFF will prevail, and that the pledges given to Lord LANSDOWNE by the Russian Ambassador will be respected. It is possible that the news from Port Arthur may not exercise a pacific influence at St. Peters- burg. The Russian destroyer which arrived at Chifu from the beleaguered fortress was evidently sent on an errand of special urgency. Her officers gave a highly optimistic account of the garrison, which, according to them, was holding its own and in the best of spirits. The story does not coin,.lide with the information furnished by independent observers with the besieging troops. It does not seem very likely that General STOESSEL sent these messengers on such a desperate venture with despatches full of assurances that he can hold Port Arthur for an indefinite period. Probably he has intimated to his Government the precise limit of his endurance; and to all appearance this means that the gallant commander is in very dire straits. When the Russian destroyer had performed her mission to Chifu she was blown up in the harbour ' It was not a very correct action for a belligerent in a neutral port; but it is interesting chiefly as an index of the Russian situation. What happened to the destroyer at Chifu may happen under a still greater pressure of necessity to the remnant of the Russian squadron in the harbour of Port Arthur. It would surprise nobody to learn that, having sunk his ships, General STOESSED had decided to surrender.He will have done .enough for the prestige of the Russian arms, for a more courageous defence of a fortress is not recorded in military history. As for the prospects of relief, he must be well aware by this time that they are hopeless both by land and sea.

Unity of a practical, workable kind, between the forces of Liberalism and Labour is one of the things which we have ardently advocated for a considerable time, and it therefore affords us especial pleasure to announce that such a desider atum from the Progressive point of view is now realised an important extent in Warrington. There has been formed this week a new political organisation with the attractive title of the "Warrington Progressive Labour League," and at its head are many of the most influential and respected labour leaders in Warrington. It need hardly be men- tioned that the League is entirely independent of and apart from the I.L.P. and the L.R.C., the local branches of which have frequently put forth unsubstantial claims to be the mouthpieces of labour opinion in Warrington. The League is a genuine workingmen's organisation with true Pro- gressive ideals—which will be found stated more fully in another column. It should go far to bring about the consolidation of local Progressive forces and the return of the Liberal-Labour candidate, Mr. A. H. Crosfield. We hope the enlightened labour men of Warring- ton will see the absolute necessity of an early alliance with an organization possessing such promising potentialities.

Of the many hopeful signs which the Liberal workers of Warrington view with increasing con- fidence, none afford more encouragement than the whole-hearted enthusiasm and loyalty to the party' which have been so strikingly manifested of late both in Warrington and throughout the country - Further evidence of the healthy stlte of affairs locally, was afforded by the large attendance and extreme cordiality which marked the concert and distribution of bowling prizes held at the Liberal Club on Thursday evening. The success of the gathering more than realized the rosiest expecte- tions,and the presence of a good sprinkling of the fair sex stamped it as quite unique in the annals of such functions. Mr. A. H. Crosfield again showed his keen imterest in all that conduces to the popular' ising of healthful recreations by distributing the prizes and delivering an interestinc, and thought' ful address. The social character of the meeting precluded any lengthy political discourse, but in the brief time at his disposal Mr. Crosfield was able to remind his hearers of the splendid causes which they will be called upon to fight for when the Govern m3nt's day of reckoning comes. His announcement that in all probability Warrington will in the near future be honoured with visite from such eminent politicians as Mr. Lloyd George and Mr. Winston Churchill evoked great enthusiasm, and there is sure to be a big muster of Free Traders to welcome these able exponents of the principles which have done so much to create and maintain our commercial supremacY; Liberal prospects in Warrington never lookeh brighter and in Mr. Crosfield we have a standard- bearer who will worthily uphold the best traditions of the party.

Mr. A. J. Jolley seems to be sore from the effects of his recent struggle in Town Hall Ward, else! surely, he would not have used the extremelY intolerant language which fell from his lips at Or Board of Guardians meeting on Wednesday. IN e have for some time noticed in Mr. Jolley an increas- ing impatience with criticism, and his attitude towards those who have ventured to differ from him on matters of public policy is much to be deprecated. Wisdom is one of those treasures which all men possess in a greater or lesser deg.rea and we would caution Mr. Jolley against setting himself on too high a pedestal. The Propert, Owners' Association was quite within its rights ine protesting against the proposed expenditure 01 £7,000 in building extensions. The members Lir° men who feel the pressure of high rates sooner than anyone else and they very properly resent Mr„; Jolley's charge of a deficiency in intelligence humanity. Furthermore it is'not the Guardians duty to pay no attention, as Mr. Jolley puts it, "to the claniour of selfish ignorance frow out- side." The opinion of their constituents Is. 5 thing that they cannot ignore without forfeiting the trust reposed in them. Mr. Jolley is an ab.le man, but it ill becomes him to talk in this strain when matters are not going entirely his own wf!Y,,' He only provokes people to charge him wit' being" wiser in his own conceit than seven Men that can render a reason."

-- Mr. R. A. Naylor's candidature for Carnarvon Boroughs has always been looked upon in War; rington as rather a joke. but it is getting beyonu that. In fact it can only be described now as 3 musical comedy. Mr. and Miss Naylor have 311 been opening a political meeting at Conway Wit' a vocal duet consisting of the following quatrain, which the lads of sleepy, out-of-the-world Nevin' are reported to be singing every night : " He has nailed his colours to the mast, Just like a fighting sailor; They are nailed well; and will stick fast

For they are nailed by Naylor. The dainty, Naylorian hand, is evident in the COW'

,

position of this charming verse. No one could rival the poetic intensity of such a line as: "They are nailed well; and will stick fast" but the famous author of " Nugpe Ca.nore and a thousand hymns. Perhaps the new duettists will favour a Warrington audience with a selection: Then we shall have an opportunity of estimating. what effect their novel electioneering is likely to. Feave on Mr. Lloyd- George's hard-headed ctiv- ,stituents.

'The important proposal to utilize the lay-by on the Manchester Ship Canal at Walton is to receive -the attention of a special committee appointed by -the Chamber •of Commerce. There seems to be little doubt that if a guarantee of an adequate .return is forthcorping, the Canal Company will be willing to provide the necessary accommodation -for the storage of cargoes prior to shipment. As far as the local traders are concerned the question is purely one of 2s. d. Would it pay them to dispatch and receive goods through the lay-by, in preference to making use of the alternative routes of communication ? At present many cargoes -for Warrington manufacturers are sent past our .very doors to Manchester and from thence barged -to Rowley, while export goods are conveyed by river and rail to the ports and transferred to the large vessels. Of course, to firms possessing barges of their own the utilization of ths lay-by -would not offer great advantages. We see no reason, however, why other traders who happen to be less favourably situated in that respect should not benefit materially by a system of direct ship- mAnt to and from Warrington such as the new scheme would furnish. The committee will inquire into the subject in all its bearings, and we hope the ultimate result will be an arrangement whereby "Warrington traffic along the waterway will be developed to the increased prosperity of local industries.

Mixed metaphors are often as amusing as the most sparkling wit. Members of Parliament and of local authorities seem to have a special aptitude for putting the cart before the horse or doing something equivalent and equally strange with their metaphorical allusions. Only yesterday, Alderman Forshaw of Preston distinguished him- self greatly in this direction. He described the Ribble as "a proverbial dog in the manger. Everybody kicked it." We are told that the Council laughed. Whether the Ribble or the dog in the manger was kicked appeared equally amus- ing° We should imagine that the latter would retaliate by biting, however, and the laugh would then be "on the other leg,"—tolfollow Alderman Forshaw's metaphorical example.

The Rev. Morley Stevenson sounded the right note in addressing the Mayor and Corporation on Sunday when he pictured an ideal of government and put forth a plea for a true realization of citizen- ship on the part of every thoughtful inhabitant. His broad and well-reasoned utterances are worthy of the earnest consideration of local legislators and all who have the best interests of the community at heart. The rev, gentleman is by no means oblivious of the difficulties which beset governing bodies, in fact he enumerated pressing problems which sooner or later will have to be faced. Tne safeguarding of the public health is in itself a matter of serious concern especially in view of the continual migration from the country to crowded town and city life. It is encurnbent upon the responsible authorities to do all in their power to secure for the poorer classes proper houses with efficient sanitation and whole- some surroundings so that, in the words of the preacher, the future generation may not grow up "a stunted, anemic, bloodless people." Without the co-operation of the individual citizen, model conditions of town-life would be impossible of attainment, and we are glad the preacher empha- sised this sideluf the question. If Corporations and burgesses would work zealously with the one ideal of making their particular municipalities brighter and happier, a decided improvement would be assured.

"A movement is on foot in Yorkshire, which, it is said, promises to become a national one, to appeal to the Board of Education to include swimming in their list of grant-earning subjects." Thus runs a modest news paragraph in one of the dailies. Apparently the widespread idea that all children should be taught to swim is at last tak- ing practical shape. We should imagine that there will not be much opposition to the movement, for almost everyone can recollect some instance where want of knowledge of the art of natation has resulted in loss of life that might otherwise have been saved. There is more necessity in our opinion for the compulsory teaching of swimming than there is for, say, compilsory military service, on behalf of which there is a strong agitation nowadays. S3lf-defence is a natural law with all animals, and with most of them swimming also comes instinc- tively. Man, however, is a remarkable exception. Unless taught, he is unable to do what every animal he sees around him can do from birth, that is, support himself in water. But the question does not need arguing on these lines. The necessity is for some practical scheme whereby a knowledge of swimming may become universal, and we think the movement in Yorkshire is one that should unhesitatingly be supported all over the kingdom. It appears a feasible and reasonable plan fnr remedying, so far as this country is con- cerned, a lamentable human deficiency.
