CHESHIRE EDUCATION COMMITTEE

The Dismissal of Teachers.

ALLEGED CLERICAL TYRANNY.

The Cheshire County Education Committee which met at Crewe on Monday, with Mr. C. E. Thorneycroft in the chair, again discussed the subject of teachers' agreements. Canon Maitland Wood, of Runcorn, secre- tary of the Chester Diocesan Schools Association, had written a long letter to the Education Department setting forth that the agreement approved by the County Education Committee virtually put the teacher into direct communication with the officers of the authority over the heads of the school managers. It further stated that in cases of notice of dismissal being given to a teacher with the consent or by direction of the local education authority, the fact should be stated on the face of the notice, but when notice was given by the managers "on the grounds connected with the giving of religious instruction," then, not the fact, but" those grounds shall appear on the face of the notice." It seemed to the Associa- tion that for the local education authority to insist on such a point was not only to open the way to undesirable discussion of theology and to make it extremely difficult for the managers to use the right of dismissal reserved to them in the Act, but that it was a direct interference on the part of the local education authority in religious matters outside their province. Dr. Hodgson said that Canon Wood's letter was an unfortunate misrepresentation of the position of the Committee. The Committee had to consider how far the teacher would be protected. He denied that the Committee had any desire to override the legitimate rights of managers, but it was their duty to give the teachers fair play. They had just had a case of scandalous clerical tyranny. The Chairman appealed to Dr. Hodgson not to go into that question now. Dr.'Hodgson said he was not going to be put down. The Chairman objected to the use of the words clerical tyranny. Dr. Hodgson replied that he should use stronger words when he came to deal with the subject. Judge Bradbury interposed later with a suggested amendment of the county agreement which, he thought, would satisfy both the committee and the Diocesan Association. He moved that any directions to the teachers should be conveyed "throulii the managers." That, he thought, would dispose of one difficulty as to dismissals; and he suggested that where notice of dis- missal was given by the managers to teachers on religious grounds, such grounds of dismissal should be communicated by the managers to the committee forth- with, and the teacher could apply to the education authority and get the reasons. Mr. J. M. Yates, K.C., thought that that offered a very reasonable solution of the whole difficulty. The Rev. W. H. Binney said they wanted the question peacefully settled, and Mr. Alderman Beeley said that was what they all wanted. The amendments suggested by Judge Bradbury were passed.

The Cork County Council has resolved to present an address of welcome to Mr. O'Donovan Rossa on his arrival from America on Saturday. Rossa was sentenced •to penal servitude for life in 1867 far his connection with the Fenian rising in that year, and was released ome fifteen years ago in a general amnesty.

LOCAL NEWS.

Coming Events. • The People's Services in the Parr Hall will be con- tinued to-morrow evening when the Rev. H. Dunnico will take for his subject Does the grave end all? If not what is beyond ?" Miss Plinston w;ll be the soloist. _ _

The preachers at Bold-street Wesleyan Church to-morrow will be, in the morning the Rev. J. Gunnell and in the evening the Rev. J. Manning. The annual sale of work in connection with the Baptist Church, Latchford, will be held in the schools on Thursday and will be opened by Mrs. Ziba Armitage. Plans for the concert to be held in the Parr Hall next Saturday night, will be open to-day. The concert the proceeds of which will be devoted to the District Nursing Association, will be given by" The Japanese Serenaders" who make their first appearance before a Warrington audience. On December Ist "The Japenese Serenaders "will again give a conc3rt in the Parr Hall. On this occasion violin solos will also be given by Mr. William Henley the renownei Eng- lish violinist.

The annual meeting of the Warrington and District Nursing Association will be held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Tuesday evening. At the meeting the work of the Association during the past six years will be reviewed in a paper which will be read by the superintendent. Local iron and steel workers will hold a public meeting in Thewlis-street schoolroom to-night at 6.30, at which the speakers will be Mr. J. Cox, of Darling- ton, Secretary of the Union, and Mr. A. H. Crosfield. Warrington's annual gymnastic display will be held in the Gymnasium on Moaday.l special feature will be introduced.in the shape of weight-lifting and wrestling competitions for amateurs. Adulterated Jam " Growers tell us that the price of fruit is so low that it does not pay to market it. We should have thought so favourable a circumstance would have allowed such a margin to jam-makers that they could afford to give us purity. But cases are constantly cropping up which prove, we assume, the contrary.. For instance, a few days ago at the Warrington Borough Police Court, a tradesman was summoned for selling jam containing 31 grains of salicylic acid to the pound. A. statement of the manufacturer claimed that all makers used the acid as a preservative.' We are glad to say it is not So, and we quite c )ncu.r with the prosecution, who urged that "the public were defrauded, and also that the acid acted as an irritant on the stomach. If jam was properly made no pre- servative was required. "The defendant was fined. '— " Sc ience Siftings."

The usual fortnightly meeting of this society was held in St. John's schoolroom on Tuesday evening last. Mr..W. E. Bindloss, of Stockton Heath, gave a paper entitled "The Picturesciue East,—Personal Experiences." Mr. Bindloss pointed out how vastly superior were the means of communication with the East at the present time compared with what they were in the sailing ship days. He went on to give a highly interesting account of life on ship-board, describing the Suez Canal, Poi t Said, Aden, &c. He referred at length to Bombay, Calcutta, and many other places in India and Ceylon, giving vivid details of life in each. The paper was much appreciated by those present and a hearty vote of thanks was accorded Mr. ilindloss. At the nest meeting, on November 29th, there will be a series of papers by the members on "Holiday Rambles."

After Eighteen Years. At Crewe, on Monc:ay, Walter Sutton, baker, of Warrington, was charged with assaulting and desert- ing his wife, Margaret Sutton, of Crewe. The prosecutrix stated that her husband, after violently assaulting her, left her two years after the marriage, which was eighteen years ago. Recently she accidentally met him in Liverpool, and on Saturday he was arrested. The prisoner, who said that he thought his wife did not want him, was. ordered to contribute towards her maintenance.

Sixth Asylum for Lancashire. It is stated on reliable authority, that the Lunacy Commissioners have approved the plans prepared on behalf of the Lancashire Asylums Board by the county architect for the proposed asylum at Whalley, to be erected on the detached "villa " system, as opposed to the prevalent single-block style.. The advantages of the system are neither in first cost nor directly in administration. Ecmomy occurs through the grad- ing of patients andfthe consequently shortened period of detention. The W nalley Asylum will be the sixth owned by the Lancashire Asylums Board, the other five being at Lancaster, Whittingham, Rainhill, Prest- wich, and Winwick respectively. Actress Drives "Like Lightning." At Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesday,Miss Kitty Lord, 120, Great Portland-street, London, W., a well- known actress who is now performing in a leading production, was fined £2 and costs for having driven a motor car without having a licence, and £5 and costs for driving it at a dangerous rate. The con- stable described the, speed at which Miss Lord was driving along the High Legh road as "most furious and reckless" and " like a flash of lightning." When she stopped in response to Fs signal she was asked for her driver's licence, and said she had not got one. She said : "Don't make any mistake ; I shall be awfully disappointed if I don't get a sum- mons."—Mr. A. Browne said Miss Lord was only holding the steering wheel whilst her friend who owned the car was smoking his cigar,

At the Manchester Assizes on Wednesday, a special jury heard an action in which the plaintiff, Mr. George Edward Aleyl-Dia, °Warrington, sought to recover £334 7s. 6d. commission on the sale of certain shares from Mr. Herbert Walmsley of Preston. Mr. Horridga, K.C., with Mr. Acton (instructed by Cruesemann and Rouse), were counsel for the phintiff, and Mr. Langdon K.C.,and Mr.Mansfield(instructed by MrP.H.Edelston) appeared for the defendant. ' The claim was for half profit alleged to have been realized on the sale of certain rubber shares. The defendant denied that he ever agreed to pay such commission, that the sale of the shares was negotiated by Iplaintiff, and that he (Walmsley) agreed to pay half profit on certain other shares, on which, however, no profit was in fact realised.—On behalf of the defendant, Mr. Langdon said that prior to the date upon which the plaintiff was alleged to have commenced negotiations, Walmsley had an opportunity of disposing of his shares. He submitted that it was improbable that he would offer commission to Heyl-Dia to do what he could accom- plish himself at any time.—After a lengthened hearing the jury found for the plaintiff for the amount claimed.

LIBERAL-LABOUR ALLIANCE. Warrington Progressive Lab Nip League Formed.

INAUGURAL MEETING LAST NIGHT.

An important development in the field of local politics took practical shape last night when an in- augural meeting was held at the Liberal Club, of" The Warrington Progressive Labour League." There was a good attendance and it was unanimor.ly decided to form the Leagae with the primary object of securing the return at the next Par"amentary election of the Progressive candidate, Mr. A. H. Crosfleld. The other objects of no League are (1) the defence of free trade; (2) resistance to Chinese or any other form of slave labour in any other part of the British dominions; (3) the establishment of a- national education scheme; (4) defence of trade unions and the protection of their fun's; (5) amending of the House of Lords; (6) improved land laws and taxation of land values, especially in and near cities and towns; (7) -justice to Irelend; (8) improved liquor laws; (9) army reform. The League will work in conjunction with the War- rington Libeial Association. The'officers on the suggestion of Mr. J. Howes were elected for a term of three months, to be eligible for re-election at the expiration of that period. They were : Mr. J. Howes, president ; Messrs. W. Haigh, Elwell, and T. Barnes, vice-presidents ; 'Mc-ars. W. Haigh, J. Ellwell, T. Barnes, J. Willett, Knowles and the Rev. H. Dunnico, committee; Mr. E. Osborne, hon. treasurer; hon. secretaries, Messrs. J. A. J. Morton ad J. Howes, jun. The League will meet every Monday evening com- mencing next Monday week. All inquiries should be addressed to Mr. J. Howes jun., at the Liberal Club.

PICTURE POST CARDS.

The series of pictorial post cards issued by the London and North-Western Railway in August last, showing the development of the railway during the last seventy years, have been exceedingly popular, over half a million cards having been disposed of. A revised and improved edition is now ready, which contains many more features of interest, including views on the Liver- pool and Manchester -.3ilway in 1829, the London and Birmingham railway in 1837.01 d and new Royal saloons; land old locomotives and carriages are shown in con- trast to the latest productions of Crewe and Wolverton. The cards can be obtained at the stations, town offices, hotels, stationers, in the dining cars, and on the steamers belonging to the company.

THE VALUE OF RECREATION.

On rising to return thanks,Mr. Crosfield was received with loud cheers and the singing of "For he's a jolly good fellow." He said he desired to thank Councillors Tinnion and Wilkinson for the , kind things they had said and to thank the company very cordially for the extremely hearty way in which' they had received him At those meetings it had been their practic3 to enjoy the fine arts and to discuss politics at the same time. Their programme on the present occasion, which was quite a red letter day for the club—(hear, hear)—was so extensive and included such attractive items that he thought it would be a mistake for him to dwell at any length upon political questions. He would have the opportunity of doing so on Saturday evening and also next week when, he was glad to say, their dear old friend Mr. Broadhurst was coming down to give them another speech. (Applause.) He was sorry to tell them that Mr. Broadhurst had had trouble at home which had caused him a great deal of anxiety during the last year or two,buthe(Mr. Crosfield) saw him in London last week and in spite of theitrouble he was as full of fight and vigour and energy as ever he was. He (Mr. Crosfield) hoped that Warrington Free Traders would come in great numbers to the Parr Hall to give their old friend the heartiest welcome he ever had here. (Applausei He had also been in touch with Mr. Winston Churchill and Mr. Lloyd George—(lond applause)—and he had as good as got A PROMISE FROM MR. CHURCHILL

that he wo—ulti-Come to Warrington before the next Parliamentary election. (Applause.) He told their friend Mr. Lloyd George that he thought it would not be a bad thing if he carried the war into the enemy's quarter, and as his opponent hailed from Warrington he had better come down and pay them a visit. Mr. Lloyd George said he had every expectation of being able to come and speak to them in Warrington during the next Parliamentary Session. (Applause.) With regard to the proceedings of that evening, Councillor Tinnion, whom he was so pleased to see present, had stuck to the Liberal party not only at the present time when everything was telling in their favour, but when circumstances were very different—(hear, hear)—and were tolerably depressing for a good many years as regarded those who were responsible for keeping the Liberal end up in Warrington. He had to thank Councillor Tinnion and Councillor Wilkinson for what they i had so kindly said. He had often talked about billiards, bowls and outdoor recreations to the members of the club and he had never either in that club or in addressing other gatherings recommended any of those recreations as an end in themselves,but always as a means to an end.(Hear, hear.) He ventured to do that now, and he could assure

them that although it was not much he had been able to do to promote those ADMIRABLE OUTDOOR RECREATIONS for the working classes, as long as he lived and had the means he would under all circumstances continue to do as he had done during the last twelve or fifteen years. (Applause.) The King made a remark some time since about music that struck him (Mr. Cros- field) very much. He said he thought there was nothing that helped to bring different classes together and did more to promote fine feeling amongst all ranks of the community in England than music. He (Mr. Crosfield)thought that in England we might class with the refined and delightful recreation which they had in music as one of those things that brought them all to- gether and prom ted a fine feeling in the country, those very outdoor recreations he had just alluded to. He need not labour the point, blotuse the advantage of it was obvious to them all. They could work better if they kept themselves in what Emerson called the "top of condition" by those outdoor amusements and recre- ations. Apart from work there were so many other things that in these crowded hours of the present day had claims upon them, and so many things that with advancing civilization, offered better opportuni- ties for the happiness of the people, or, if he might quoteMr.Morley'slivords"helped to;make men and women happier, and happier in better ways." He would just mention in illustration of that the increased demand on our public libraries. There were a great many people who demurred a little at that and regretted that so many novels were read. He did not think they need feel very ' uneasy about the amount of NOVEL READING that went on in the country, when they considered that such a distinguished and conscientious man, and such a great literary genius as Mr. John Morley had admitted that he himself was a veracious reader of novels. And then the novel reading led them on to other forms of literature. They came to poetry, and let them consider for a moment the wealth of beauty and the amount of refined pleasure that was open to them all in the study of the English poets Merle: He di cl not mean to say thztt they need take it too ssrionil;. Tie' need not read through what was called The drowsy, frowsy poem called" The Exc irsion Writ in a manner which is my aversion •as was said by a very caustic critic of the lake podtsr. Then some of the poets were a little bit mystifying. He might quote them a few more lines from the same critic about a poet called Lord Thurlow whom he had never read and whom he expected very few of his hearers had ever read. The critic's summary of Thurlow's poems when they came out abent q century ago was

- When Thurlow his said nodSenge Sant-a (T hope I am not violent), Nor gods nor men knew what he meant. (Laughter.) They need not study poetry of that kind. (Hear, hear.) There was, however, a great deal of very SIMPLE AND BEAUTIFUL POETRY by many of our distinguished writers, and to his mind there was not the slightest doubt that as time went on and as education told more and more, our poets and authors would be studied more fully. He was sure, also, that more people would make excursions into that fairyland and wonderland which was being opened tt; them by science every year that passed. Then there Wafi history. ,He did not know whether any of them had read the Warrington press this week. If they had, they would understand that he would have to tread a little warily in speaking of history, because his brother had advised him to study his history books a Little more closely before continuing the discussion they had on hand. (Laughter.) He would like to say a brief word about music, because he had taken a great interest in it ' all his life, and it was one of his favourite hobbies. They knew that at Bank Quay their works were taking a very active part in the development of the musical faculty amongst the employees of the concern, and no doubt they knew that the band wa; very successful at the Crystal Palace not long ago. (Applause. y He would have the pleasure of assisting at a celebration of that success at an early date. If literature offered a great variety of culture and relaxation, he thought there was nothing more refreshing and no more perfect form of 'refined pleasure than they gut in music. Although he had been at that club many a night and had listened to many concerts which had been a great treat, they had never—and he thought probably all his friends present would agree with him—had a concert in which they had listened to music so perfect and high class, and which had been altogether so delightful, as the programme which had been submitted that even- ing. (Applause.) They had had numerona successful concerts, but, to use a colloquial phrase, he was sure that one fairly beat the record. (Applause). It was perhaps an indication of the general trend of opinion— if there was any political importance to attach to it— in this town as throughout the . country. He was not going to allow that remark to lead him on to discuss politics, but they must not think it was because there was not plenty of material. The difficulty for anyone who got on a platform nowadays to talk about politics was to know what to leave out. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) They almost suffered, as Mr. Morley said not long since, as a party from being over-capitalised. But it was a good fault. and in truth

THE POLITICS TIAEY HAD TO DEAL WITH now were far above any mere party issue, and far beyond any mere question of a gain, as it was some- times called,between the ins and the outs. (Hear, hear.) They had problems of the deepest kind, .that would take pretty well a generation—twenty or twenty- five years—of the hardest and most strenuous work that the progressive party could put in if things were to be put on a right footing and if a real, earnest effort was to be made to raise the standard of civilization, to give the masses of the people a better chance and also to wipe out those plague spots upon ouricivilization—which he thought must be the chief object of every patriotic Englishman and certainly of every Liberal politician (Applause.) The whole thing was being brought well before every voter in Warringtou. They had a great .undertaking on hand just now, of which they would hear more and more as the weeks went on, and he could assure them as far as Warrington Liberals and he himself were concerned, whether the Government delayed the election a little bit longer or not, whatever hankey-pankey tricks might be tried they would have to face the music one of these days—(hear, hear)—and he could assure them that when the occasion arose they in Warrington, as elsewhere, did not mean to fence and skirmish any longer. (Applause.) They would come to close quarters. They had always felt they had a good cause behind them, but they felt that with ten- fold conviction nowadays, and they would figlit solidly and whole-heartedly together. (Applause.) In conclu- sion he thanked them very much for their kind recep- tion. He was sorry if the effects of the last ten weeks had a little bit touched his voice, but they must not think he appreciated any the less the kindness which had been expressed and which he always received, not for his own deserts but because of family traditions, within the walls of the club. He hoped the club might continue to prosper and that their bowling competitions might be a great success year by year. (Loud ap- plause.)

WARRINGTON LICENCEE'S SUICIDE.

A sensation Ras created in Warrington on 'Monday, when the news gained currency that Mrs. Elizabeth Plinston, the well-known licensee of the "King's Arms' Inn," Buttermarket-street, had committed suicide. Mrs. Plinston, was the widow o the late Bugle Major Plinston of the Ist Volunteer Battalion (P.W.V.) South Lancashire Regiment, and on the death of her husband succeeded him in the business which she for many years conducted in a creditable manner. The facts of her suicide were detailed at an inquest which was conducted by *r. Brighonse, at Warrington on Wednesday.

Frederick Plinston said the deceased who was 58 years of age, was his mother. On Sunday night she was rather strange and depressed in her manner. On Monday morning she called the household to get up for business. About eight o'clock,witness heard her vomit- ing and on going into her room found her in a state of collapse. He sent for Dr. Peacock who on arrival pro- nounced life to be extinct. In her bedroom witness 'found a jug which he gave to Dr. Peacock. For some time she had suffered from !Its of depression and on one occasion said she was afraid she would do something to herself whilst in those moods. Dr. Peacock said he had made a post-mortem examina- tion of the body. The mouth, throat, and internal organs pointed to the presence of some corrosive acid such as oxalic acid, a solution of which was contained in the jug which was handed to him by the last witness. The jury returned a verdict of "Suicide whilst of unsound mind" and expressed their sympathy with the family in their beeeavement. THE FUNERAL.

The funeral took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Thursday, and was attended by a large number of sorrowing relatives and friends. The service was con- ducted by the Rev. H. Cook. The chief mourners were Mr. and Mrs. J. Plinston (son and daughter-in-law, Mr. and Mrs. David Plinston (son and daughter-in-law , Mr. and Mrs. Kermode (daughter and son-in-law, Messrs. Edward, Fred, Charles, and Valentine Plinston (sons), and Miss L. Plinston (daughter). The Licensed Victualler's Association, of which the deceased was a member, were represented by Messrs. T. Walsh, J. , Whittle, P. Brimelow, Massey, Teece, and McClinton. I The sergeants of the Ist Volunteer Battalion (P.W.V.) South Lancashire Regiment, acted as bearers. Floral tributes were sent by Mr. and Mrs. J. W. Plinston 1 Mr. and Mrs. IL Kermode, Mr. and Mrs. D. Plinston 1 Messrs. Edward, Fred, Charles, and Valentine Plinston, ; Miss Lizzie Plinston, Miss E. Plinston, Mrs. S. Plinston , and family, Mr. W. Pear Son, Mrs. Mullen and family, ' Sergeants of the Ist V. 8., L.L.R., Mr.and Mrs. Thomio- son, Mrs. Cliffe. THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of science is such as will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest -discoveries of the age, while they have proved directly beneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, injured 'other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to remove one class of disease, or suit some constitutions, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is some scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the pre- valent diseases of this country, which will be certain to do good when fairly tried, which will be equally adopted to the needs of the merchant and the workman, the professional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand, the miner, and the roadside labourer. It should be, too, such a pre paration as contains.no injurious ingredients, and which ' may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the stronger constituted man. Such a discovery would deserve- to be called The Perfection of Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved, it has been found satisfactory. WARRINGTON ESPERANTO SOCIETY. Letter of Coniia—tulationlfrom a Pioneer of the Movement.

Besides many, in Warrington and district, who are privately studying Esperanto, there is already a mem- bership of about thirty enthusiastic ladies and gentle- men who form our local Esperanto Society, and who meet on Monday evenings (7.45) at the Old Academy. Mr. W. El. Woodcock, the first president of the Society, has received what—from the point of view of Warring- ton intellectual interests—may be considered as an historical letter of congratulation and encouragement, in regard to the recent very successful establishment of our local Esperanto Society. • The letter, of which we append a copy, is from Keigh- -157 (yorits). written by Mr. Joseph Rhodes, founder of the +first Esperanto Society in this country, and an eminent British authority on the new international language.

To Mr. W. FI. Woodcock, President, Warritvton Esperanto Society. Dear Sir —I was not much surprised, though delighted and encouraged, to learn that the correspond- ence on Esperanto in your local press has led to the initiation and nropagation of theauxiliary international language. Permit me to offer you on behalf of your membership, the hearty congratulations of our own Society up in this step, and to give you our best wishes for the success of your efforts. You will meet with obstacles, and possibly, opposition, but you will learn to prefer the latter to contemptuous indifference. As to your difficulties, which I am glad you have so frankly stated, I may reply (1) that the nn-English look of Esperanto is du.) to the fact that there are a few hundred million people of European civilisa- tion who are not English, and they have to be considered, in any language that pretends to "inter- nationality." Familiarity with Esperanto breeds, not contempt, but a growing admiration for the genius with which Dr. Z tin 3nhof has found the line of lest resist- ance, among needs and tendencies which,to the ordinary mind, prove irreconcileable. After referring at some length to the use of inflected adjectives and pronouns in Esperanto, lir. Rhodes continues :—No, be content at present to believe—what personal investigation is bound to confirm—that, although no human device is perfect, Esperanto has been made as perfect as, under the peculiar and many-sided conditions of the problem, any language, artificial or natural, may ever be expect- ed to be. It is in this particular. far superior to any living tongue, and vastly so in comparison with any system of artificial langna,ge ever yet invented, or—with much knowledge on the subject, I will venture to say— ever likely to be. I am a vlry busy man, whose leisure moments for a very long ime have been devoted to Esperanto, and the heavy d • a Inds of the larger English-Esperanto Dictionary, upon which I have been engaged since the Spring of 1902,prevent my doing very much active pro- paganda outside the conducting of our own Society's elementary and advanced classes on Friday nights. I do not know what night you meet on, but if I coJd manage to run over on some convenient occasion, I should be pleased to see you, and perhaps do more by a few words, viva voce, to clear up your difficulties, than is possible on paper. As to the, speakability" of Esperanto, dismiss every fear. This August I spent a fortnight in Belgium. Although I can read French, with tolerable facility, I cannot speak it, and had to depend entirely on Esperanto. It proved in credibly easy, and adequate for all purposes. This the second holiday I have spent abroad, dependent o Esperanto, and the experience has been.the same both'

times. I have spoken wit h eight different nationalities and there has not been as great a difference, in pro- nunciation, as between an educated Lancashire man and a Londoner.

Kun Koraj Salutoj JOSEPH RHODES (Espo 5260).

Mr. J. C. O'Connor, author of the gsperanto text book and grammar, has also sent congratulations and good wishes to our new society.

BIRD SHOW AT WARRINGTON.

THE PRIZE LIST.

The Ninth Annual Show of cage birds and homing pigeons promoted by the Warrington and District Cage Bird Society was held in the Conservative Club Assembly Rooms on Friday and Saturday last. The show was opened on Friday afternoon by Mr. Henry Roberts IP., who complimented the members on their splendid exhibition. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded him for officiating. The entries in the pigeon section num- bered 150 and in the cage bird section over 200. The chief local winners in the pigeon section were Messrs. E. Burrows, Warrington, D. Shakeshaft, Lymm, J. Ward,Newton-le-Willows, and H. Craske and Son, Sankey Bridges. In the cage bird section the chief honours were carried off by Mr. J. D. Leigh, Warrington, who with 6 entries in thu open classes won 4 first, 4 specials, 1 second and 1 third. The show was well attended and is likely to prove a financial success. WARRINGTON AND 7.11 E SHIP CANAL.

SUGGESTED UTILIZATION OF THE WALTON LAY-BY.

Meeting of the Chamber of Commerce.

Important business, including the proposal to utilize the Walton lay-by on the Manchester Ship Canal for th. development of the local trade, was under considera- tion at a general meeting of the Warrington Chamber of Commerce held at the Town Hall on Monday. Mr. John Fairclough presided and there were also present : Messrs. C. E. Parker (representing the Penketh Tanning Company), F. W. Monks, (Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co. Limited), W. aLong (Messrs. W. Long and Son), H. Kingdon and T. A. Mosso (Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons Ltd), E. L. Fleming(Warrington Borate Co, Ltd), L. Henshall(Mr. R.A. Naylor),J. H. Smethurstrassrs. J. Smethurst and Son), C. D. Parkinson Uessrs. Tunstall and Co) 0. Lofthouse(Llngford Wire o. Ltd) H. Gibson (Messrs. Mackie and Co. Ltd), W. R. Gray (National Telephone Company) and Alderman Bennett (hon secretary).

The SECRETARY announced that he had received letters regretting inability to attend from Air. J. Charlton Parr,Hr. J. Fulton Smith.and Alessrs. Fletcher Russel/ and Company. TELE AUDITORSHIP.

The CEIAIRNIAN moved the appointment of :%Ir. C. D. Parkinson as auditor in place of the late Mr. Thomas Tnnstall. Mr. LoNG seconded. Alderman BEssErr rernarkei that ever since he (the speaker) became connected with the Chamber the late Mr. Tunstall undertook the duties of auditor and did the work efficiently. The Chamber was most indebted to him and to his firm for the kindness they had shown in the matter for many years past. The proposition was unanimously agreed to. NOMINATION OF REPRESENTATIVE ON THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

The CHAIRMAN said that Mr. Fulton Smith had hitherto acted as representative of the Chamber on the Education Committee but. he lrid now been elected a member of the Council and appointed a member of the committee by that authority. It therefore rested with the Chamber to nominate another representative. The Council ha-. 1 discussed the matter and recommended the nomination of Mr. H. Kingdon, and he (the chair- man) moved that their action be confirmel. _ _

Mr. SMETHURST seconded. Mr. LONG said that as art old member of the Council he strongly supported the recommendation. He believed that the more the younger members were interested in educational work the better it would be. Alderman BENNETT remarked that fora long time Mr. Kingdon had been one of the most active members of the Council and had taken the greatest interest in the work of the Chamber. He had also been keenly interested in the Education Committee's work, more particularly on its technical sida, and Messrs. Crosfield were co-oparating very warmly with the committee in their endeavour to make the education suited to the needs of the locality. He (Alderman Bennett) was sure Mr. Kingdon would be an acquisition to the committee. The nomination was unanimously approved.

THE AFFILIATION OF TRADE ORGANIZATIONS. NEW RULE.

Alderman BENNErr said a short time ago the Grocers Association made application that they and similar trade organizations might be affiliated with the Chamber. The matter had. been Carefully considered by the Council and it was thought they would strengthen the Chamber and increase its representative character if, on suitable terms, they arranged for the affiliation of societies tof the character mentioned. After consider- able discussion a new rule was drawn up to deal with thel matter. He had been in communication with the Secretary of the Sheffield Chamber of Commerce (Mr. Herbert Hughes) who said a similar rule was in force there and had worked very well. After some discussion the following rule was adopted :—Any local society of a commercial or indus- trial character may be affiliated with the Chamber on payment of an annual subscription of two guineas which shall entitle each such society to nominate one member to attend the general meetings of the Chamber as its representative. TFIE SHIP CANAL LY-3V AT WALTO.N. SUGGESTED DIRECT INIPORT AND EXPORT TRAFFIC. Alderm in BENNzrr intrelacal the qaeetion of the pr vowed utiliz ',den of the lity-tv oa the Manchester Ship Canal at Walton. He hid, he said, been approached by one or two members of the Chamher and several gentlemen who were not connected with it who thought local traders might make more use of the lay-by. He understood that the Manchester Ship Canal Company. would be quite willing to provide facilities if they thought they would be recoupel by substantial traffic. The gentlemen by whom he had been consulted seemed to think the best way to go about the matter was to appoint a committee to inquire into the question in all its bearings, to find out the rates of carriage by the various routes to Warrington with the object of ascer- taining whether any saving might be aff cted by using the lay-by. Perhaps it was one of his numerous fads, but he had always thought that on the face of it there ought to be a saving. He knew as a matter of fact that some classes of goods for Warrington were conveyed up the Ship Canal to Manchester and then barged back to Howley Quay. Obviously, it ought to be cheaper to discharge the cargo at Stag Bridge rather than pay the extra carriage to Manchester and back. If the committee found there was a definite saving, the Chamber might approach the Canal Company to induce them to provide a shed for the storage of goods and other facilities. Mr. Fleming. took a great interest in the proposal and he had perhaps special opportunities of judging as he had recently erected works near the lay-by and had received some of his raw materials direct by the Ship Canal. 'Mr. Parker was also concerned in the shipment to Warrington of a cargo of peat from Belfast which was satisfactorily dealt with at the lay-ey and. it was thought, with a saving. Mr. FLE3IING said he had gone into the subject very carefully and he fon id that if the London, Glasgow and Belfast steamers would stop at the lay-by, there would be a saving to the traders of 2s. or 3s. per ton, as against the cost of sending goods by barge to Liverpool and shipping'them there. The vessels would not call at Warrington for small parcels of goods, and it rested with the community generally to aggregate sufficient quantities—say 50 tons—t justify a stoppage. If it was definitely arranged that coasting steamers should call at Walton on a certain day each week, the steamers for foreign ports might follow their lead. He had personally had the assurance of one of the Spanish lines that they would stop for as small a parcel as ten tons. He thought it was reason- able to suppose that if manufacturers cartedltheir goods to Walton and shipped them it would be cheaper than conveying them by barge to Liverpool and paying the Mersey Docks and Harbour Board dues before the cargoes could be exported to coast or foreign ports. It was even more expensive to send goods via Manchester. The Ship Canal Cempany had stated that if the people of Warringtoa would come forw trd. and really utilize the lay-by, they would go to the expense of erecting a shed for the storage of goods. Ta 9 lay-by had already cost the company £4,000 and the crane £2,000 but nobody used them. The company did not, therefore, feel inclined to build a shed unless they hada guarantee from the traders, merchants, and manufacturers of Warrington that it would be taken advantage of. Alderman BEINETT read the following letter on the subject received bya local firm from Mr. Herbert Gib- son, chief traffic superintendeet for the Canal Company: lam in receipt of your letter of the 10th inst., and note with interest the suggestion you make that this company should construct a shed at the War- rington lay-by in which traffic from various War- rington shippers might be concentrated until sufficient quantities were collected to warrant coasting and continental steamers calling there to take the traffic direct on beard and so save the cost of lighterage or railway haul to the Manchester docks or to Liverpool. It has always been our policy to give very careful consideration to antimatter of this des- cription which might result in increased traffic to the steamers trading to and from the canal, and if we could be sure that the Warrington traders would take advantage of such facilities we should be quite willing to lay ourselves out to receive traffic at the Warrington lay-by and to provide the necessary accommodation. , I am afraid, however, that without such a guarantee we could not see our way to incur the necessary outlay. In this connection I would respectfully point; out that this company went to considerable expense to pro- vide adeep water lay-by at Warrington, equipped with , transporter for loading and discharging vessels, and we ' were led to believe that Warrington traders would largely avail themselves of those facilities. Up to the present, however,as Foe will be aware, the traffic handled at this lay-by has been of a very meagre character.

loss said that from fignres he had seen, it appeared to be quite impossible that the lay-by could be utilized at a saving to the manufacturers. ,If the Canal Company could give figures showing that anything could be saved, his firm would no doubt be willing to take advantage of the facilities. _

Mr. MoNtc said it was cheaper for his firm to send goods via Liverpool. Mr. FLEMING : You have your own barges. The CHAIRNIA.N said that as far as his firm were con- cerned, they found it cheaper to discharge imports at Runcorn.

Mr. FLEAtrsc: It is the firms who have not barges and other facilities that I am considering. Mr. LOFTEIOUSE pointed out that thousands of tons of steel rods for Wafrington were conveyed to Man- chester and then barged to Howley Quay and carted to the works. In the case of that kind of material every extra handling added to the cost. Surely it must be cheaper if the goods could be brought to the lay-by and carted into town.

Dr. Adams reported that the outbreak of scarlet fever continued. In Stocktnn Heath there had been three cases, and in other parts of the district there had occurred 9 cases. REFORiVIEB EPISCOPAL CHURN.

Advanced Ritual in the Chulech of

England.

A meeting was held in the Co-operative Hall, Winwick-street, on Monday evening, in connection with the Northern Council of the Reformed Episcopal Church. Bishop Eldridge presided over a small attend- ance and was supported on the platform by the Revs. Dr. Bowman (Liscard), Dr. Naish (Gunnersbury), J. R. Parker (Tuebrook), F. L. Goode and F. Vaughan (Warrington), and Messrs. T. Allen and W. Dickens (Warrington), S. Reece, A. Reddington, T. Wilson, H.T. Thorpe and W. Mason (Liverpool). The Ciia.IRNIAN explained that the meeting was,held as it was thought it might he helpful to those mem- bers of thi. Reformed Episcopal Church in Warrington. It was also held with the object of removing any mis- understanding which might have existed as to the prin- ciples of their church, and he earnestly hoped those two objects would be realized. Bishop Eldridge proceeded to explain the origin of the Reformed Episcopal Church. Bishop Cummins the founder of their Church left the established church because of the' sacerdotal ritualism existing in the church and because of the uncharitable- ness and exclusiveness of the Church of England. Bishop Cummins felt his conscience could no longer allow him to continue in a church so exclusive and he therefore formed another church upon broader lines —a church in which they adhered closely to the Church. of England in liturgy, church government and order, while at the same time recognizing the existence of other sections of the church of our Lord Jesus Christ (Applause). Dr. Nlisx remarked that they were a protestant, liturgical, and evangelical Church claiming to be pre- eminently loyal to Christ and the religion of our Lord Jesus Christ. They desired to teach the people the Work of God as contained in the Bible. They did not expect to bring about unanimity of creed, but they desired to have a unanimity of beliel—a unity of the spirit in the bond of peace—and to draw nearer to Christ by cultivating more and more the spirit of prayer. (Applause.) Dr. BOWMAN said he was attracted to the Reformed Episcopal Church by the purpose which the founders had in view—the maintaining of the great principles of the Reformation, principles which were indestructable as they were divine. If there ever was a time when there existed a need for the advocacy Of the principles of the Reformation and the enforcing of those principles, it was the present time, when theology of the Church of England, which was S 9 popular, was diametrically opposed to the true principles of a Protestant Church. At the present time there existed in the Church of England a state of things which was truly lamentable. In that Church they found the mass was lauded as a means to grace and that the confessional was advocated and practised as a help to theidevout. In this church of ours we had an Estab- lished Church which was sapping and undermin- ing the great truths found in the word of God, and which were the basis of the salva- tion of the human race. In the Church of England there was a process of disintigration going on and they found the errors which were practised, though perhaps not advocated openly, were frequently advocated by means of circulars privately issued amongst the members of the parish. They existed for the combating of those evils, and hoped for the sym- pathy of the people in their work as they were conscious of the righteousness of their cause. (Applause.) Mr. H. T. THORPE also addressed the meeting. During the evening two anthems were ably sung by the choir of Emmanuel Reformed Church.

===== 0002601_19041119_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
MIDLAND IRON AND STEEL WAGES BOARD.

The special sub-committee of the Midland Iron and Steel Wages Board, which on October 24th issued circulars inviting outside firms to join the board, met on, Thursday at the Queen's Hotel,Birmingham,under the presidency of Sir Benjamin Ringley to receive the replies. These were found to be numerous, and on the whole are considered to be fivourable. A large propor- tion favour the continuation of the Board. A special report is in course of preparation for submission to the meeting of the Standing Committee which on December Ist will fix the rate of wages for the ensuing two months. Far the present, it has been decided to restrict the missionary efforts of the Board to South Staffordshire, Shropshire and South Wales these being considered to be more properly within the board's area than the other districts. The further exten- sion will be a subject of future consideration. This decision, it should be explained was taken on the initiative of the Operatives' section who contended that the Board should put its own house in order before seek- ing to make converts. The settlement of the matter is complicated considerably by the fact that in many of the firms appealed to the workmen are in no way asso- ciated with the Board, and leading members of the Association are not disposed to recommend their followers to join until they are satisfied that the terms of the proposed sliding scale generally meet their re- quirements,ror there is a hope of the Board becoming a representative and authoritative body in the trade.

MOTOR NOTES. -

Although it is rather late in the season for the attempt considerable public interest is being taken in Captain Deasy's present reliability run. Captain Deasy intends to cover no less than four thousand miles on his 16-20 h.p. Martini, travelling at the rate of two hundred miles per thy, and returning each evening to the Automobile Club premises in Piccadilly whence the start each morn- ing is made. An official observer is carried every day, and it is his duty to record the causes and duration of all involuntary stoppages, so that at the termination of the trial the public will be able to gather from the pub- lished reports exactly what may be expected to occur with the mechanism of a good car upon an ordinary tour under ordinary touring condition.. The state of the engine and the tyres at the conclusion of the run will also be carefully noted.

•Illeferring to the matter, a contemporary remarks that Captain Deasy is a pioneer in so far as having his car officially observed throughout the trip, and thinks that the public, being accustomed by now to more or less romantic accounts of monster runs during which no trouble whatever was experienced, will not quite appreciate the new conditions and may not be disposed to give full credit to the Martini.

All motorists who attack their own repairs know:from unpleasant experience how very difficult it is to keep the hands in nice condition. The dirt clings particularly in cievices around the nails and this gives one's hands a moat objectionable appearance. But a doctor, who is also a motorist of mechanical ability tells us that if the cracks and creases in the hands, and especially the interstices around the nails, are filled up with soap before the repairs are commenced, the dirt will not work through and the customary washing after the work is completed will leave them smooth and soft.

The use of gloves when working on the car is not to be recommended since they destroy that delicacy of touch which is so necessary for most of the adjustments and repairs undertaken; but if the hands be first treated with soap in the manner suggested, the result in appear- ance will be equally as satisfactory as if gloves had been worn. Many motorists wash their hands in patrol or paraffin before invoking the aid of ordinary soap and water; but although this certainly does remove the dirt very efficaciously, it roughens the skin and thus renders it far more susceptible to being subsequently stained or soiled.

At the time of writing the Stanley Show is on the eve of opening. Next w.eel:: we shall hope to give a short resume of the principal and most interesting exhibits.

SOCIETY AND SUNDAY.

Rev. J. F. Matthews and Marie Corelll's Article.

The Rev. J. F. Matthews preached in the Baptist Church, Latchford, on Sunday evening, from the text "The Sabbath was made for man, not man for the Sabbath. The Son of Man is Lord also of the Sabbath," and during his address referred to Marie Corelli's article in the" London Magazine" for October, 1904 on " Society and Sunday." Taking a high vantage ground he said and looking over the whole area of English life, one was struck with the prevailing attachment to Sunday as a day of rest, to the growing opposition to Sunday pleasure and Sunday labour, together with a saner view of its purpose and utility. Dealing with the following passage from Marie Corelli's article. "For reason is as much the gift of God as speech, and to kill one's intellectual aspiration in order' as so-ne bigots would advise, to serve God more com- pletely is the rankest blasphemy. The wilful refusal to urea great gift merely insults the giver." Mr. Matthews said the writer was almost, if not entirely wrong. His complaint was not against the modern preacher not using his reason or intelligence—be he Anglican or Nonconformist. He should rather be disposed to ques- tion the attitude of the average reader of the Bible, the average listener to sermons. There people generally speaking, did not bring the same lively search- ing intelligence to bear upon their reading of the Scriptures as they did upon the newspapers, or as they exercised in their business. He quite admitted the hesitancy and over-cautiousness of many preachers, but did not the reason for that lie in the fact that most churchqs were creed-bound, and that preacher § were compelled to think within the limits of the prescribed and formally stated belief? He was not underrating the intelligence of those who had drawn up the creeds, nor was he questioning the founda- tion truths they were trying to defend; but be contended that with the growtlikof knowledge many statements in the creeds need modification, while the correct- ness of some was seriously open to doubt. In the third portion of her indictment,Marie Corelli accused the clergy of partiality. "It is,says she, "by obstin- ately declining to branch out, as .it were, of the great tree of Christianity in forms which are not narrow or limited, but spacious and far-reaching, that the clergy have in a great measure lost much that they have retained. Society has slipped altogether from their hold. society sees for itself that too many clerics are either blanant or timorous. Some of them bully; others crawl. Some are all softness to the wealthy, all harsh- ness to the poor. Others, again, devote themselves to the poor entirely, and neglect the wealthy, who are quite as much, if not more, in need of a 'soul cure' as the most forlorn Lazarus that ever lay in the dust of the road of life." There was probably more truth in that statement than any of the others quoted. The reasons were obvious. A church bound hand and foot to the State, which lacked autonomy even in spiritual affairs whose very Prayer Book was the codicil to an Act of Parliament, was not one to encourage outspoken- ness in the clergy, while those of high degree, who frequented courts, and assisted at courtly func- tions, were oftener than not smooth-tongued, mealy- mouthed sychophants—the last people in the world to denounce the Paganism, the lust, the laziness, and the vice which infested society throughout. The inferior clergy, those who entered the church as a profession and not for the love of men, with their eye everlastingly upon fat livings in the gift of private patrons, or colleges, or the Crown, were not prepared to risk their chanches of preferment by faithful dealing .with un- scrupulous brewing companies for instance, whose "trade" took precedence of all things else in their own view, or by denouncing the ill-gotten gains of rich men. In conclusion the Rev. J. F. Matthews pleaded for a recognition of Sunday as the Lord's day, an apostolic lagacy,to be accapted and used as a sacred and happy trust. He asked that we shall accept it as a stewardship to be held for the purposes of the highest enjoyment, and rest of mind and body. It was the first day, the week's preparation day,wherein,f reed from the usual amcations and exactions, man breathed a purer and serener air, and was thus braced and strengthened for the work of the days that followed.

===== 0002601_19041119_art0093.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

WIPE BEATER AT WIDNES.

At Widnes on Thursday,Peter IVlorris,residing in Ditton was fined 20s. and costs or one month, and bound over to keep the peace for six months, or one month in de- fault of finding surety, for assaulting his wife Charlotte on Saturday last. The woman's left jaw was black with the blow, and she said that it had happened before, but she had never taken any proceedings.

NEw METHOD OF BEGGING.

A curious method of fraud was revealed at Runcorn j Petty Sessions, on Monday, when Henry Newton' a middle-aged man, of Morecambe, was sentenced to two months' hard labour for collecting alms, in the follow- 'lug manner. He was in the habit of delivering from house to house pamphlets on the unemployed, problem, I with titles such as the "Tradesman's Lament,' in which were set forth the distress which has been prevalent in different parts of the country through want of employ- ment. Afterwards, trusting to hick that his pamphlet hal been read by the householder, he would call upon ; him and tell a very pitiable story of the plight in I which his family were placed through his want of work, and in this way obtained various sums of money. TERRIBLE PALL.—MERSEY TRANSPORTER BRIDGE ACCIDENT.

I The first accident of any serious note during builling I operations on the new Widnes and Runcorn transporter bridge occurred about three o'clock on Wednesday afternoon, when a married man named Herbert B. Marshall, residing at the Crimean Tavern, 13, Regent. street, Runcorn, lost his life. Through the snapping of a wire which was being used in the completion of the jointure of the sides over the middle of the river, I .Marshall was precipitated into the water below a distance !of about 120 ft. He was alive when picked up by means 1 of a boat, but expired shortly before reaching the shore. The body was conveyed into Runcorn.—The bridge is ! almost nearing completion, and the large staff of men I were working on the last girder which would complete the bridge in the middle. The news created consterna- tion in the town, where the deceased was well-known.

THE RECENT TOWN HALL WARD

ELECTION.

" A State of Things Much to be Deprecated."

The first of a series of smoking concerts in connec- tion with the Junior Conservative Club, was held on Monday evening at the Club. Mr. E. Draper presided over a large gathering. 111 the course of a brief introductory address the Chairman alluded to the by-election in Town Hall Ward decided last week. The municipal elections had be- come a matter of history and he was afraid this year he could not congratulate Conservatives on the satis- factory nature of the results. But there was one feature in connection with the elections which he con- sidered rather more unsatisfactory than the results them- selves. He referred to the Town Hall Ward election in which two independent gentlemen at enmity with one another thrust themselves forward and took such a prominent part that the candidates themselves appar ently had to take a back seat. He though- it was very much to be deprecated that suet a state of things should have existed, for sejleraY reasons. One was that anybody who wished to take an part in that election .was placed in a somewhat false position. He went down endeavouring, as he would do, to assist the Conservative candidate, and instead of being able to do so he found himself, whether he willed it or not,placed in the position of having to fight under the banner of one or the other of those two gentlemen, although that was the banner under which he had no inclination nor any desire whatever to serve. He could quite understand the voters themselves would very much resent the fact that they could only exercise the franchise an behalf of practically one or the other of those gentlemen. He trusted that in future those two gentlemen would restrain their exuberance until such time that they would have the election all to them- selves as duly selected candidates, and then he Was sure they would all be pleased to watch the fun. ( Langhter, and hear, hear.) Mr. W. P. RYLANDS addressed the meeting at length on the question of our fiscal policy taking as his text " large imports indicate prosperity of the investing or moneyed classes and large exports indicate prosperity of the working-classes,' and advocating Mr. Caamber- lain's fiscal proposals.

ALTRINCHAM v. LATCHFORD. At Altrinchani. In the first half Altrinchara did most of the pressing and at half-time were leading by two goals to none. In the second half the home side had things all their own way, and thanks to the two outside men Ferguson and Skelton, won by 7 goals to nil.

CHESHIRE VICAR KILLED. Distressing Wagonette Accident Near Northwich.

--- A distressing accident occurred on Wednesday after- noon at Acton Bridge, Northwicb, causing the death of the Rev. D. Lyon Bennett, aged 56, vicar of Little Leigh, Northwich. The vicar, his wife, and servant were being driven in a small wagonette to Acton Station. The horse took - fright, the vehicle overturned, and Mrs. Bennett sus: tamed a dislocated shoulder. Mr. Bennett was found to be unconscious. He was placed under medical attention, but died at three o'clock from severe concussion and other injuries. He was exceedingly popular, and was one of the best-known vicars in Cheshire, having acted at Little Leigh for about a quarter of a century.

BILLIARDS. THE STROKES OF THE GAME.

Eli .gCio 1,1E.-NTI.

[COPYRIGHT.)

RUN-THROUGHS.

Of all the strokes in the game of billiards, perhaps the one which presents the greatest diffi- culty to the ordinary player (leaving the muse out of the question) is the run-through, es- pecially when the stroke is an in-off. The ordinary player will now and then, more by "luck than anything else, bring off some extremely difficult pot which would be a really good shot for a Stevenson or a Dawson, but when it comes to an ordinary run-through in-off, which any really good player would practically never miss, he almost invariably fails at the stroke. Unless, however,, a very fair mastery of these run- through in-offs be gained, players will, owing to the frequency with which positions demanding the run-through occur, seldom make big breaks. In this chapter on run-throughs, I therefore in- tend to discuss both simple and difficult strokes, and amongst the latter will be found several strokes quite unknown to all except first-class players_ A.M 27

DIAGE

A run-through in-off. Red ball touching the cushion and 18 inches from the pocket, 'cue ball behind the spot and 6 inches from the cushion.

Diagram 27 shows position for a run-through into a. top pocket, the red ball being dead on the top cushion about 1.8 inches from the centre of the pocket, and the cue ball bcing directly behind the spot, and mid-way between the spot and the cushion. Such a position is typical of what may be termed the simple run-through in- offs. ' I don't mean to say that this in-off is exactly an easy stroke for a player who has never made much over a 20 break, but for a run-through in-off the position could not be better, and a good player practically never misses such a stroke.

As a rule it will he found that the players who constantly miss such a stroke, miss it not because they fail to make their ball run through the object ball but because their ball instead of clinging or running very close to the cushion, travels away from the cushion. For anyone who is weak at run-through in-offs there can be no better practice than the stroke shown on Dia- gram 27. Five or ten minutes' practice every day for a. week should enable any player to get this run-through time after time. Everything however. depends upcn the manner of !,;riking the cue ball. Great care should be taken that there is nothing jerky about the stroke The cue should he held as horizontally as possible and the cue ball struck with a free, flowing stroke; that is to say the cue should follow on some distance after striking the cue boll, if the action of the cue be at all jelly or the butt be appreciably raised, the tendency will be for the cue ball to come away from the cushion in- stead of clinging to it all the way to the pocket. Aim should he taken to strike the object ball just a shade less than full and the cue ball sle3uld, of course, be struck on the side which is near the cushion (right hand in the Dia- grant) and well above the centre. Thereis, how- ever, not the 4lightest necessity to try and hit the cue ball at the very top. Half-w;ly between the centre and the ,top is quite high enough Many Players play the run-through shown on Diagram 27 at high speed, relying on the top which they impart to their ball to carry it into the pocket. But though the stroke can be made easily enough this way, this method is not the one by which run-throughs of the nature of the one under discussion should be played. A strong stroke wauld in all probability leave the red, in baulk, and even if it did not go into baulk it might easily run safe. A medium-pace stroke. is all that is necessary, and this will cause the ob- ject ball to travel more or less as shown by the intersected line on the diagram. Thus by caus- ing the red to travel to somewhere near the Pyramid spot or other point on the central line of the table, position for an in-off into one or other of the top pockets will always be left for the next stroke.

In practising this stroke the distance between the balls should be varied, and the object ball should occasionally be placed farther away from the pocket.

DIAGRAM 28,

A rnn-through in-off. Red ball 1 inch from the cushion, and 18 inches from the pocket, cue ball behind the spot and 6 inches from the cushion.

Diagram 23 shows a position rather similar to the cne just discussed, the difference being that the object ball is a little away from the cushion. Mary players who are fairly goad at the in-off wiwa the object ball is touching the cushion generally miss it when the ball is a little away from the cushion.

This run-through, with the object ball away from the cushion, must be played in -exactly the same manner as when the ball is-touching .the cushion, with the exception that a ball which is away from the cushion must never be struck quite as full as a ball which is touching the cushion. No definite rule can be given as to how full a ball which is away from the cushion should be struck as the required degree oft fulness will vary with the angle which the two balls make with the pocket, but a very little practice of the stroke will soon determine the kind of contact necessary for its accomplishment. Should the cue ball strike the cushion very soon after con- tact with the object ball, the contact has been too full.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Emmanuel Church.

Special services were held at Emmanuel Church on Sunday, in celebration of the anniversary of the dedica- tion of the church, and were.attended by large congre- gations both morning and evening, and also at the special service in connection with the men's union in the afternoon. The Right Rev. Bishop Eldridge preached on each occasion. Special music was given by the choir, under the conductorship of Mr. J. Lloyd. Miss Gandy presided at the organ.

Warrington P.S A

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The usual meeting was held on Sunday, when there was a good attendance. Mr. Mills presided, and referred to the argument advanced by some people that P.S.A. meetings werd only places of amusement. If those critics would come and see and hear for them- selves, they would, he was sure, be convinced that there was something uplifting, as well as pleasing in their gatherings. He had always found the beautiful hymns, prayers and addresses, as well as the reading of God's Word, inspiring to himself, and he could also speak for many more, who had had a like experience.—Mr. Coyne, of Harpurhey, was the speaker, and gave a stirring tem- perance address.—Miss Plinston rendered "Sun of my soul" in a pleasing manner. Mr. A. Healey and Mr. C. Cotton also took part in the meeting. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ.—The Secre- tary welcomed four new members, making a total of 556. He also proposed a vote of sympathy with the families of two older members who had passed away.

===== 0002601_19041119_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
Mersey-street Mission Formed.

For some two years some of the members of the Temperance Mission Lodge of the 1.0.G.T. and a few friends held " cottage meetings "in various parts of the town, and found such good work was being done that it was deemed advisable to open a mission room in which to hold regular services. A suitable room has been rented in Mersey-street and it was opened last Sunday. In the morning a short service was held for the workers, conducted by Mr. F. Merlin, who gave a brief address on the words "Watch and pray."—The preacher in the afternoon was the Rev. J. F. Matthews, who remarked that the mission should be a great power for good in the district. Speaking upon temperance work he said they might think the outlook was very gloomy, but in spite of the Government having passed an iniquitous Drink Bill the grace of God was able to win men from the evils of intemperance and make England what, it had never absolutely been, sober industrious mid God-fearing. In conclusion he urged the members to be united, and not to allow the mission to be a one-man or a one-woman affair but to allow everyone to have a share in the management.—A large congregation assembled in the evening when Mrs. Moores gave an excel- lent address on Repentance and Conversion. The following officials have been elected :—President, Mr. P. Bate; treasurer, Mr. F. Merlin; secretary, Mr. C. J. Sheath; stewards, Messrs. T. Hughes, J. Brand- wood, S. Bishop, T. Morgan, and J. W. Ludgate.

===== 0002601_19041119_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
St. John's Presbyterian Church.

The Sunday school anniversary sermons were preached at St. John's on Sunday last, by the Rev. D. Wilson, of INVigan. In the evening Mr. Wilson chose for his text the words "Render unto Cmsar the things which are Cwsar's ;and unto God the things that are God's"( Matt. 22nd ver. 21). The preacher called attention to the circumstances under which the words were spoken. The Pharisees asked Jesus if it were lawful to give tribute to Cwsar. It was one of those questions which looked innocent but meant mischief. If he answered " yes "he offended the people, and if he answered "no " he ran foul of the civil power. The answer given was characteristic of Jesu§ in that it was applicable not only to the case in point, but to similar cases in every age. The principle was one which held good in their own day and which appealed to every man's sense of right, justice and fairplay. No man could charge Jesus with anything that was incon- sistent with good constitutionalism. In their own case Cwsar was the king or whoever was at the head of the State. The man who ruled and the man who was ruled each had rights, and it was to the welfare of both that these should be honestly and fairly acknowledged. If they dealt honestly with men, they ought to act in the same spirit towards God. Did they render unto God the things that were his? Did they pay the tribute in full without any rebate or reservation. It was right that a man should make up his mind to get wealth, but was it not folly for him to give his whole time and energy to worldly things. neglecting the one thing needful? Let them be "diligent in business, fervent in spirit, serving the Lord." The anthem "1 will lift up mine eyes" was rendered by the choir, while Miss Jowett sang the solo "Calvary." Mr. J. NV. Jowett pre- sided at the organ. Collections were made on behalf of church and school funds.

===== 0002601_19041119_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Missionary Work in China. The Horrors of the Boxer Rising.

The large congregations which assembled in Wycliffe Church on Sunday, bad the pleasure of hearing exceed- ingly interesting addresses on missionary work in China by Dr. Sewell McFarlane. The occasion was the foreign missionary anniversary, and the offertories which realized a very satisfactory amount were handed over to the London Missionary Society. The Memorial Hall in Edgeworth street was well filled on Tuesday evening when Dr. McFarlane related the thrilling story of his escape from the Boxers dur- ing the rebellion in 1904.—The Rev. John Yong° presided and explained that although Dr. Mc Farlane was a medical missionary in the great empire of China they must not forget that the society which he represented carried on its work in a great many other parts of the world. Dr. McFarlane was born in the South Sea Islands, where his father was a missionary in those days. His father, the Rev. Dr. MacFarlane, who was now living retired at Southport, after a long life of service, was the first missionary who ever set foot on that great and yet unconquered island for Christ—New Guinea. Whilst on furlough about thirty years ant:. he visited Wycliffe, and spoke with such effect that the school made up its mind that it would support one native teacher, at any rate, in New Guinea. In those clays of courset they had greater power of ra sing money than was possible to them at the present time and thanks to the generosity of Mr. Murray who was then superintendent of the school and promised to double any amount which the scholars might raise, the handsome sum of £l4O was forwarded to the society that year. The Society carried on work in India where Wycliffe had its representative in their friend Arthur Dignum who, aftera shoi t furlough, was now engaged with renewed earnestness and success in the work to which he had given his life. Towards the funds of the London Missionary, Society they raised, in Warrington for the year ending in 1901 a little over £7O, in 1902 £72, in 1903 £Bl, and 1904 a little over £76, They were anxious that this year they should send at least one tenth more than they contributed last year. And he was glad to know tat the col- lections on Sunday had increased to about that extent. If the society was to do its share towards evangelising the world it must send out without delay fifty more missionaries. At the present movement they had not money to send out one in addition to those already in the field. He appealed to all present to contribute as liberally as possible. Dr. MCFARLANE said his subject was one that he would rather talk about than go through. The news- papers made a great mistake when they said the Boxer risinglwas an anti-missionary row. It was not an anti- foreign row, and was no more directed against the missionaries than against traders and members of the possessions. China was riddled through with secret societies, and the Boxers, who com- prised one of them, were simply agricultural labourers who were out of work. The lecturer pro- ceeded to deal with the commencement of . the Boxer rising, showing that thelßoxers after murdering several missionaries became emboldened. On June 21st when the troops had been ordered to another station and the town at which he (Dr. McFarlane) was stationed was unprotected, the rebels metand made a solemn vow that before sunset the following day they would murder all the missionaries in the compound. Dr. McFarlane gave a thrilling description of the flight of the missionaries and their families and their providential escape from massacre. During the rebellion many of the native Christians lost their lives in the most terrible way. Some were made to dig their own graves ann were then pushed in and buried alive. Others were hanged on trees, strangled, shot, burnt, and beheaded. One native woman seventy six years of age who had been a Christian for twenty years was dragged into the street by the hair and surrounded by Boxers who beheaded her. In conclusion the lecturer said the Church in China was stronger to-day than ever it had been. It had turned out all the "bread and butter Christians" and they had now a solid church. More workers were urgently required, and he asked all present to support the society to the best of their ability. The Rev. JOHN YONG E expresied the thanks of the meeting to the lecturer, and remarked that he would not be satisfied until they at Wycliffe gave to missionary objects in the same degree that they paid for amuse- ments.

A collectioti was taken on behalf of the society.

Mr. Rise, Levi will be pleased to answer in these columns all questions relative to the article appearing Weekly, and as far as possible any questions on other points in connection with the game of Billiards. Envelopes should be marked "Billiards."

NOTES ON THE ROBERTS v. REECE MATCH. This match which was played last week in Manches- ter at Messrs. Ornie and Son's saloon, created a very ' great amount of interest,play each session being before , a full house. Reece played a good game all through the week, his top-of-the-table play being of a very Ugh ' order and his touch in nursery cannon play being , wonderfully delicate. It was Roberts however, that the public came to see and they were rewarded by see- ing that" grand old man "give some marvellous exhi- bitions. It was not so much the size of the breaks, although many of the breaks were over 200, that delighted the spectators hut the fascination consisted in the variety of his strokes and the rapidity of his play. Other professionals when they get a bad position carefully weigh it up, change their mind once ,or twice n ? and the play the difficult stroke with extreme care. Not so with Roberts. He had made his stroke { before the majority of the spectators knew what he was ' going to play for. On the Saturday night he played and made a most difficult steeplechase shot almost the second the three balls had run into the bad position along the cushion. I was present every afternoon and 'evening, and though I have seen John Rot-erts play I often before the fascination and r beauty of his I play never appealed to me more than it did last week. I I would rather watch John Roberts make a hundred ; break than any other player a two hundred break, and I that this was the feeling of other spectators as well was plainly apparent all through the week. With regaid to the controversy that has been waging as to whether Roberts should receive two-thirds of the ; gate, should he play Dawson or Stevenson, I contend that he is fully entitled to the lion's share. I was , present four nights at the match between Dawson and Reece the previous week in London, and for every ; spectator who came to watch Dawson ten came to see ; Roberts. It is not a question as to whether Roberts is as good or better or worse than Stevenson I and Dawson, but whether or not he is a I greater draw. It must not be forgotten that ' Stevenson and Dawson have been so much before the public the last few years that the feeling is "Oh, I ; can go and see them anytime," whereas Roberts has been ' away so long that every billiard player 'wants to see him again at once, and many who a few years ago were only boys, but are now aspiring players, are longing to see the great play er of whom they have heard so much. And to see Roberts once is to want to see him again. There is something magnetic about the man, his land- some face, his commanding appearance and his splendid carriage as he walks round the table charm one almost as much as the wonderful style of his play. If any of the younger professionala'should be envious of Roberts's reputation and ability to draw the public, they should remember that Roberts had gained his laurels whilst • they were in their swaddling clothes. coming to the question of his play, it is a difficult thing to gauge it properly, but that he is good for some very big breaks there is not the slightest doubt. A few weeks ago every professional that I spoke to, with the exception of Mannock, considered Roberts as practically done. Mannock, however, in reply to my question as to what he thought of Roberts's chances against our leading professionals gave a laconic, but expressive answer : " He'll beat the lot of them." Many players pointlto the result of Roberts'sgame with Aiken, which was as follows : Aiken (received 3,000) 9,000 Roberts as tending to prove that Roberts is only second class { now, but Stevenson recently played Aiken, and the !result was as follows: Aiken (received 3,000) Stevenson Again, take the result in Roberts's match with Reece, which was as follows: Reece (received 2,750) Roberts and compare it with Dawson's match with Reece the previous week, which resulted as follows: Reece (received 2,750) Dawson . Roberts clearly does not suffer by comparison. ANSWERS TO CORRESPONDENTS. A.G. (Bradford).—Any of the leading billiard firms supply good cues. I cannot specify any particular firm in this column. "Cyclist " (Monte Carlo.)—l am glad to hear that you can get a game of English billiards in many places in the South of France, but regret to hear that crys- talate balls do not appear to have travelled so far yet. In Paris, however, they are becoming quite popular, in fact I had a letter a few days ago from Maurice Vignaux the world's champion at the cannon game in which he speaks most enthusiastically about crystalate balls- By the way, Maurice Vignaux. is 61 years old, yet lie won the championship { of the world only a few months ago. which proves that I age need be no barrier to the very highest skill at I billiards. _ _
