RUNGOAN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, when there were present, Messrs. A. R. Norman (presiding), Thos. Baxter, A. Fryer, J. Stokes, J. H. Davies, J. Spruce, T. Lea, W. M. Morris, J. L. Jones, G. Pilling, T.lantwis, H. Eyes, T. Wright, J. C. Chadwick G. Egerton-Warbur- ton, J. Mounfield, J. E. Linaker, J. E. Linaker, jun., E. Bretherton, J. LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONIANTS.

Authors of letters intended for 'publication must in v.ll oases seal their mains and addresses to the Editor :as a guarantee of good faith. [A RXTEPA.YER.—You have not yet favoured us with your name and address, as is customary.— " x."]

Mr. Dodds and the Rev. 11. Minnie°. Sire—My sermon on municipal work which appeared 'in your issue of Saturday last seems to have displeased' your correspondent, Mr. James Dodds. The many admirers of Mr. Dedds will no doubt be delighted to .find him in a new role. As the champion of the poor," inoffensive, oppressed and persecuted publican he will no doubt find and sphere of serviceMirether the' poor publican" is willing to accept service so gratuitiously offered is a matter I leave to his own dis- cretion. I beg to say, however, that I have never to my knowledge uttered one disrespeaf nl word about the pulrlican as an individual. On more than one occasion I have peblicly stated that . the publicans as men would bear favourable comparison with many other sections of the community.' therefore resent your correspondent's accusation because it is en- tirely unfounded and void of truth. Ift have said any- thing which—by any stretch of the imagination—might be interpreted as being" aninsulting reference to poor publicans "I am in a position to produce more conclu- stye evidercein support of it than Mr. Dodds can produce in support of more time one charge he has made against local public men individually and collectively. Three statements in my sermon have been singled out for special mention. '(1.) "That there had been too much spleen and jealouay manifested on both sides." (2) "lie had come into conflict vvieh the leaders of both parties." (3) "That rather than retain a man's friend- ship at the sacrifice of convictionor the price of silence he would prefer his *enmity." Y:11 of these are given without any regard to their pretera and ^context. Still they are correctly reported, and what is more are quite true. Mr. Dodds then says "I do not know how far this refers to me,' and immediately proceeds to give what he no doubt considered to he a knock down blow by stating that whilst "he 'had never sought my acquaintance I had twice appealed to him for help in my work." That on the first occasion he had sent a guinea but that onthe second occasion' he declined to contribute unless I would publicly withdraw an asser- tion" which was then and is now my honest opinion. Before dealing with these statements I desire to say how much I regret that a man of Mr. Dodds' education and ability should, bemeau himself by introducing into the columns of a public newspaper what was, to say the least,a semi-private matter. This however is typical of that littleness which has characterised more than one of his actions and certainly it does not reflect credit upon him. Dealing with hie first statement I can only say that I have never suggested or even hinted that Mr. . Deeds had ever sought my acquaintance. However, now that he has deliberately stepped out of his way to emphasize this, permit me to remind him that on the morning of Whit-Monday 1903, one of his family called at my address in Arpley-street. After handing me a donation of one guinea towards the funds of our Sunday school she stated that her father was desirous of making my acquaintance and wouqd be delighted to see me at Penketh. As I was at that time a complete stranger in Warrington she very kindly gave me minute instructions as to the locality. It is quite true that Mr. Dodds has been appeaciedto twice but both appeals were made on the occasion of our Sunday school anniversary. In the first instance I appealed to him at the directly expressed wish of one who was at that time intimately associated with 'him and a worker in our school. in response to that appeal I received one guinea. In the second instance a type- written circular was sent identical in all respects to those sent out to others. The circular of coerse bare my signature, because I am president of the Sunday school, as I am of .every organization connected witlethe Church. It was a pure accident that it fell to my lot to send the particular one addressed to him, the others almost without an exception being sent by the Secretary on the second occasion. Mr. Dodds declined to contribute "until (I quote his own words) I had publicly withdrawn an assertion I had previously made:" The assertion mentioned was then and is now my honest and unbiassed opinion. I venture to assert that 80 per cent. of the people of Warrington endorse that opinion. The state- ment objected to was to the effect that the non-trading agitation was ltrgely the outcome of spleen and jealousy. I informed Mr. Dodds that I should not withdraw it, that my remarks were not intended to apply to him indiViduelly and that as he was not at that time a member of the Non-Trading Association he was somewhat +supersensitive in applying the statement to himself. His donation was a matter of indifference to me, he was at liberty to keep it, which he did. Now Mr. Dodds has himself admitted that the appeal was made on behalf of a good work. It was in order to carry out work amongst the children, viz., the 'purchase of necessary books and to defray the cost of the annual trip into the country. Mr. Dodds therefore decided to boycot an admittedly worthy work simply because I declined to withdraw an honest criticism passed upon a public organization. What then is the 'logical deduction of such an action. if I were inclined to be hyper-critical I should say -that Mr. Dodds has scattered his donations not because he was of a charitable disposition or had a becoming desire to help on a good work but for the sake of gaining adherents to his own party and opinions on the one hand and of silencing public criticism on the other. I sincerely hope that this is not so, but his action and words suggest such a thing. At any rate he ought to have been the last person in the world to quarrel with my assertion. I repeat now for Mr. Dodds' special benefit that rather than retain a man's friend- ship or donations at the sacrifice of conviction or the price of silence I court his enmity and spurn his guineas. I refrain from dealing with other matters for the present because I have no personal desire to prolong what is likely to become a personal quarrel. Still if Mr. Dodds persists and desires a battle I am not unprepared. It is' however, senseless to waste valuable time in foolish bickerings when so much remains to be done. If Mr. Dodds will accept advice from a much younger man than himself he will cease monopolising all the critcisms passed upon any organization with which he may happen to be connected. Further he must learn to recognise that such organizations have not the sole agency for honesty and that there are hundreds of men outside of such who have not yet bowed the knee to Baal.—Yours truly. HERBERT DUNICICO.

Sir,—ln your last Saturday's issue I sought and sought only to defend myself against a pulpit charge of having been inconsistent in supporting a publican non- trading candidate for our Town Council in one Ward, while condemning teetotal Liberals for supporting a Tory brewer in another Ward. It was not my desire to provoke controversy—quite the contrary—but I learn that the Rev. H. Dunnico made a further attack upon me in his last Sunday night's sermon, and was applauded for his strictures upon my alleged refusal to help his anniversary collections. As this is at least the third occasion upon which this reverend gentleman has misrepresented me in his pulpit, I haya decided to set out once for all, the extent of my connection and relations with him, and to publish the whole of the correspondence which has led to his attacks. On Whit-Sunday 1903, I received anappeal on behalf of his anniversary services, and in a written .postscript the Rev. H. Dunnico offered to wait upon me and ex- plain more fully his financial needs. As is well known to most of the heads of religious bodies in Warrington, who apply to me,l deal promptly with such applications In harmony with this invariable practice I immediately a.cknowledged that of Mr. Dunnico, and sent him a guinea by one of my children, who was riding into Warrington on the following day (Bank Holiday) together with a verbal message (in response to his offer to call upon me) that I should be glad to see him at any time. The appended correspondence covers all my subsequent dealings with Mr. Dunnico. The first and third of his three letters I may say, are wholly in his handwriting thesecond is type-written, but is signed by him and is addressed to me by him. I have only to add my pro- found concern that there should be among the people of Warrington those who appear te have approved and do approve of the degradation of public worship by the introduction, where they cannot be challenged, of miserable and paltry personalities which are as entirely -out of place as they are altogether discreditable to that preacher as well as to the church and congregation Which tolerate them. This is my last word in a really disagreeable and unsought wrangle. In leaving this correspondence to speak for itself I would merely poin::, out :- 1. That I subscribed to Mr. Dunnico's church funds. 1 He accused me or the party to which I belonged with being actuated by spleen and jealousy. • 3. T refused his application for a second subscrip- tion until the insult had been withdrawn. 4. Mr. Dunnico then threatened to shortly expose me.

5. Mr. Dnnnico has now asked his questions "in a more public manner."—Yours truly, JAMES DODDS.

November 17th, 1904.

[COPY.] Baptist Church, Golborne-street, Warrington. May 17th, 1904. Dear Mr. Dodds,—l am holding a "United Confer- ence "to discuss the best way of solving the "Social Problem "on Tuesday evening May 29th. Representa- tives of the various Trades and Labour organizations have promised to attend and I shall be pleased to have a word or two from you. My aim in calling this con- ference is if possible to unite the progressive forces of the town without which there can be no real and per- manent reformation. The conference will follow immediately our usual service and will commence about

===== 0002601_19041119_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
S p.m.—Kind tegards, sincerely yours. ItNIIBERT DUNNICO.

(coiry] Baptist Church, Warrittgtom

Councillor Bogs, Dear Sir,---On Whit Sunday we are:to 'celebrate the anniversary of our school and church, and are making a specialelfort to raise £lOO. Ihe objects in view are as follows (a) To provide suitable books ecc, for the scholars. (b) To give the poorer children a free day in the country. (c) To conduct evangelistic and soclalwork amongst those who do not attend any place of Worship. The first two objects are clear, but with regard to the third, perhaps I may be permitted to add a word of explanation. In February last we engaged the Royal Court Theatre and afterwards the Parr Hatl for the purpose of coming in contact with those who were spending their Sunday evenings in the streets or elsewhere. The resultshave exceeded our expectations; from the outset we have had crowded congregations, and scores, if not hundreds, have been influenced for good, and have become better husbands, fathers and citizens. The expenses incurred however, are great, and our church funds have suffered considerably. We therefore appeal to you in the hopethat you may see your way clear to help us in a work which is purely unsect9.riau and undertakensolely for the common good. Apart from the eitrals`bour, time, (Sic., the movement has involved, it has also cost me personally at let £lO, and that is why I take the liberty- of appealing to you in this manner.—HERBERT DtKNICO, Pastor, 4, Fairfield-road, Stockton Heath, Warrington. P.S.—I shall be plecsed to wait upon you personally if you desire. [COPY.] Periketh House, near Warrington. May 17th, 1901. Rev. Herbert Bunnico, Warrington. Sir,--I have your invitation of this date to a United Conference, as also your appeal for financial aid in your church work; but must decline to be associated with, or to help you in any way. Last year I willingly responded to your anniversary appeal, but some little time thereufter you went out of your way; in a letter published in the Warrington "Guardian." to state (in effect) that the movement for the purification of the Warrington Town Council was the outcome of spleen and jealousy. As the leader of that movement I know your statement to be an absolute 'falsehood so far as T and those associated with me in the origin of the Non-Trading Association are concerned. Speaking for myself there is no man in Warrington in regard to whom I have ever had an ungenerous feeling or with whom I have ever 'had the slightest difference or of whom I have ever felt the Olightest jealousy. I have no unkindly feeling 'towards you, but 'until the charge you publicly made against me and those associate(' with me has been as 'publiely withdrawn,' I must decline to have any kind of 'public Association with you.—With the respect due 'you, whatever that may be, I am itc., JAMES DODDS. [Copy.] Baptist Church, Golborne-street, Warrington. May 18th, 1904.

Councillor Dodds. • Sir,—l am in receipt of yours Of the'l7th and had I anticipated such a reply you may rest assured that I should not have written you. I was under the *impression that all who honestly desired to see a better state of affairs in existence would welcome any •step which tended to unify the progressive forces of the town. I regret now that I ventured to approach you. With regard to the statement you mention, you misquote it. I used the present and not the past tense, and as you had previously repudiated your connection with the" party" you must be supersensitive to find in it any reflection upon yourself. If you had taken note of all my public actions and not singled out this onej think you would have seen them) minister in the town has spoken out more strongly against every form of impurity. The victory of Councillor Pemberton in Latchford was largely due to me ; even the opposing party admit that it was owing to my keen resentment of the action of the temperance party there that Councillor Ellison was compelled to make an apology before the "Temperance Union." No other man save myself ven- tured to defend a man who,whatever his faults had been made the scapegoat for others. I voiced on that occa- sion what everyone knew to be true but which none apparently dared to assert. In view of these things do you think that you are justified in writing so inso- lent a letter? May I ask you one or two questions. Do you think that the moral character of at least two members of the non-trading committee will bear exam- ination and are they fit and proper persons to lead a "purity " party,and if you thought fit to associate with them does not your refusal to associate with me (sic) savour of either hypocricy or pharasaism ? There are other questions which I may think proper to ask in a more public manner shortly, but meanwhile perhaps you may put my mind at rest on the following points. Before a recent meeting of the "Town Council " is it true that free whisky, &c., were provided by the Mayor? Is it true that certain members of the "Non-Trading Party" including yourself partook of some? Is it true that you came up from the refreshment room or kitchen arm in arm with a certain alderman—a man who has strenuously and bitterly opposed the party you are now seeking to defend? Yet you decline to associate with me. I regret having to write in this strain. I would far rather have had a ten minutes' chat with you but you had the choice of weapons. With regard to your subscription last year our people appreciated it. If however you think our work is no longer worthy of your assistance we do not desire it. That is purely a personal matter' and the result now is a matter of indifference to me. Your reasons however shall be placed before them. I may say,however.that I strongly resent and greatly deprecate the tone of your letter and I leave it to your sense of honour to do what you think is most in har- mony with ,your professions.—Yours &C., HERBERT DUNNICO.

[COPY.] Penketh House, near Warrington. 19th May, 1904. Rev. H. Dunnico, Fairfield-road, Warrington. Dear sir,—So far as regards the only issue between us, your yesterday's letter is about as satisfactory as I had anticipated, and although there is no relevancy whatever in the questions. you put to me, I will answer them. You ask" Do you think that the moral character of at least two members of the Non-trading Committee will bear examinationand are they fit and proper persons to lead a purity party, and if you thought fit to associ- ate with them, does not your refusal to associate with me savour of either hypocrisy or pharisaism ? My reply is: (Ist) I know nothing whatever against the moral character of any member of the Non- Trading Committee, but (2rkl) I know that you pub- lished a statement which is absolutely false. Your further questions are "Before a recent meeting of the Town Council is it true that free whisky &c.,were provided by the Mayor? Is it true that certain members of the" Non-trading party,' including yourself, partook of some? Is it true that you came up from the refreshment room or kitchen arm in arm with a certain alderman, a man who has strenuously and bitterly opposed the party you are now seeking to defend?" My answer is that so far as lam concerned these statemerts are altogether untrue.but that had they been true, as I am not a teetotaler, and as I have no personal animus against any member of the Council (as I have already told you) I cannot see any point whatever in your questions. Of course I should not opprove any such practice as that you attribute to the Mayor: on the other hand " Charity never fails." I am, &c., JAMES DODD S.

===== 0002601_19041119_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
National Service.

Sir,—l have been sent a copy of the " Examiner " for the 12th inst. in which I see that Mr. A. H. Crosfield in the middle of some too flattering reference to myself, evinces regret that I have altered my views on compul- •sory service as expressed to him in a conversation now nearly four years ago. I am afraid I must plead guilty to having modified the views I then held, but my excuse I think is a good one. It is a question I have gone a good deal more deeply into since then, and the more one knows of this subject the more infallibly must any intelligent man .come to the conclusion that compulsory service in some shape or form is to be the future safeguard of England. I have therefore great hopes of seeing Mr. A. H. Crosfield himself on our side at no distant date. The conversation Mr. Crosfield refers,to was however I think, on Conscription versus Voluntary Service, and I then held,and do still that conscription is utterly unsuited to our regular army, and that great truth lies in the old naval adage "one volunteer is worth two pressed men" providing always that that particular volunteer is as equally trained as a sailor,and as fully efficient physically and mentally as each of the two "pressed "men. Applying this to our Home Defence Army, it is ti e absolute impossibility of getting these fully trained voluntary soldiers in sufficient numbers for the Militia Yeomanry and Volunteers under any workable 'con- ditions of service that makes one turn reluctantly from the idea of voluntary service and seek some. more suit- able form adapted to modern needs. In this, one only follows in the wake of the most distinguished English soldiers of the day, who, together with many other noted army organizers, staff officers, and commanding officers of Auxiliary Forces, have searched in vain for a practical solution of the difficulty for years past. Continental Conscription is unnecessary and unsuited to English ideas, and the gnglish Ballot Act is most unfair in its incidence, but the Swiss system does all that is needful from the military point of view, and has been shown in practice to be actually beneficial to the Swiss inmany other ways. So well adapted is it to English needs that it is surprising it should not have been advocated long before the National Service League was formed, the true reason being, I think.that we were more content if possible before the South African War to "muddle on" than we are now. Anyhow a very large expert body of professional and outside opinion immediately saw the required solution of the Home Defence problem in the views advocated by the League, and again I was, and am, content to follow in their wake. AI know Mr. Crosfield fully believes that a solution can yet be found on voluntary lines, but he has the weight of expert opinion against him. At any rate if he can evoke a workable system on his lines he

will by all appearances be doing a, very great service for the present Government. Thanking you in antici- pation, I um yours faithfully, F. NI, ROOME, Major.

Sir,—May I make use of yo ar valuable spate to reply to the remarks in ray brother's letter, which was read last Friday at the meeting of the Social Service League debating class. My brother is anxious that the Volunteers should be given a fair trial and !states that they have received more kicks than halfpence. Undoubtedly the auxiliary force have not received the encouragement that might have been given. But what encouragement is going to suffice to 'induce the requisite number of men, and above all of officers, to go to camp for two or three months, which period is recognised by all competent critics as being the minimuta'that will render a man fit to meet a thoroughly disciplined foe? Surely it is fairly obvious that no encouragement will suffice unless it consist in giving sums of money so large as to put such a scheme outside the bounds of practical politics. Ought we not, however, to look upon this question of home defence from a higher point than that of mere expediency? Is it right, is it just, that A should cora- vlacently allow B to offer his life for the protection of A's own life and that of his family and belongings? In other words, is not home defence a fundamantat duty for every citizen ? I know that some people, while admitting that' every mun owes a duty to the State, think that this can be performed by personal services in other ways than by undergoing military training. There is no personal service' however, which is on the same .plane as, or can be compared to the personal sacrifice involved in submitting to the risks of war. My brother lays great stress on the danger of intro- ducing militarism into this country by raising up a national army. Let me point out that militarism is produced when a strong professional military caste gains predominance in the councils of a nation. I ask, is this likely to happen in a democratic State like ours, where the rank and file in the proposed national militia would be only temporarily withdrawn from their civilian occupation and where their leaders would 131 men of business in the first place and officers in the second? Is militarism rampant in Switzerland? Great capital is made out of the value of liberty. Now liberty is valuable for the use that can be made of it and if our young men are going to make better use of their lives by undergoing &little disciplinary training, the slight curtailment of their freedom is not only a gain to themselves individually but to the nation at large. I am very glad that my brother has put his views on this great subject in black and white. A fair oppc!si- tion is what the National Service League wants, being confidentthat its proposals are so just and necessary as to be able to withstand the assaults of any criticism that may be brought to bear on 'them. I must, how- ever, ask him to refer again to his 'history books, for the statement to the effect that the Britons who

defeated Napoleon were not compulsorily raised is somewhat inaccurate. At that period, our navy, which so gloriously swept the seas, was manned by the cruel and unjust system of the press gang, and our army which, assisted by the conscript troops of Europe, finally crushed Napoleon at Waterloo, was largely com- posed of men recruited by the Militia ballot.—l am, &c., GEORGE R. CROSFIELD. (Hon. Sec. Warrington Branch N.S.L.) Should Dancing be Encouraged in Christian Churches P

Sir,—One might almost as well ask should drinking be encouraged in Christian churches? Undoubtedly it is encouraged and strangely enough we find that it is in the services of the Churches that

drinking- intoxicating liquors is promoted. Wine drinking has ever been a favourite occupation with Christians and it cannot be denied that the Bible does not advocate teetotalism but moderation in the con- sumption of strong liquora advocates wine as a means whereby man may be made merry,whereby his heart may be made glad and light. The clergy are in false positions when appearing as apostles of teetotalism, claiming that their cause is founded on Bible teaching. And if our churches encourage drinking and have made drunkards why should they not also encourage dancing and give all Christians a good chance to become immoral? No doubt this will appear irreverent to some pious persons but what I have already said contains a great deal of truth. _ _

It is surely a proof of the ineffectiveness and failure of Christianity and Christian churches when young people in a Christian country surrounded by Christian churches cannot join together in a dance without being "led away into a very hell of sin." Are the young people of the present after nearly 200 years of Christianity so weak in character a S to fall into sin "at a Parish Dance? If this is so for goodness sake get rid of your Christianity and churches at once. What we want to do is to make the young men and women of our churches "alright "and if our churches with their Christianity cannot'do this,there remains only one course open—get something else that will make them real " young men and women." What shall it profit kumanity to gain Christianity and immorality and to lose its own happiness and the joy of its future generations ?—Yours truly, " ANTI-CFIURC LIMAN."

BAZAARS.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] Over and over again people have said and wished that there was some other way of raising money than by bazaars. Well, there are other ways, of course, which mostly concern the men-folks. Subscriptions, for instance, yearly or otherwise, the duty of collect- ing them when due and not sent in voluntarily, the somewhat novel method of "sitting to receive" gifts of money, which sounds nice and comfortable, getting up concerts, dances, entertainments, operas, &c., Whatever method is preferable it means self-denial, and in many cases hard work. As the ladies are mostly concerned in working for a bazaar, let us look at it from their point of view. When a bazaar is first mentioned they shrink from the idea and immediately think of endless garments to be mad.", weeks and months of close application, many disappointments, impaired eye-sight in many cases,and a few pleasures. But the thing grows, the men think there is nothing like bazaars for the purpose,and by and by they settle down to the idea and try to make the best of it. The first requisite is money or material, and no man surely ought to make it more arduous for his women- folks than it, of necessity, must be by being "stingy," —shall I say? "Just before generous " but nothing more. There are not many women who can afford to spend for bazaars out of their own pocket (if even they've got one) and as men or their associa- tions generally participate in the proceeds, why should they? Division of labour makes it easy— the men find the money and the women do the work-- one department of it at least—the sewing. To make that less of a task it is well to organise sewing meet- ing, at each other's house in turn, where congenial spirits may get to know one another better, get wrinkles, (not real ones I hope), as to the best way of making things, and last but not least, friendly cups of tea. By that means competition is keener, emulation is aroused, and as the garments grow one feels that it is not so bad after all.

Another department—to many people an essential one—is the begging. Personally, I dislike that very much, and if, on the spur of the moment I nerve my- self to ask for something and am not favourably received, that person is the last one I beg from. All •the same,some people are born beggars; they acquire a lot of things very easily, and I envy them rather than blame them. They contribute their .share without having done much actual work. On the other hand there are sympathetic friends who don't need to be asked—they give without—and one good turn deserves another. Whether it is justifiable to expect gifts in kind from those we do business with it is not for me to say. I don't like the principle of it, but the "cause" may take the responsibility for it. Tradesmen don't like bazaars. Though they admit that during the working period they sell much ' that is required, durfing the selling period they sell less than they otherwise would. If we were to change places with them we should doubtless think as they do. So much depends on the point of view. One of the pleasures I referred to is the satisfaction of knowing that whatever the result is you've done your best, and another is the feeling of pride, on the opening day, at the sight of so many pretty things made at the cost of much time and not a little skill. Bazaars have the reputation for making more enemies than friends. 01'! the pit, of it when it is so! It is easy to understand that there may be friction where there are so many conflicting interests, but the first aim ought to be the success of the bazaar monetarily, as that was the object we first set out to achieve. To attain that we must sell as much as we can for as much as we can, having due regard to what is considered a fair price, of course, and after that, bargains if you like, for those who deserve them, providing your little circle is all of one mind as to their value. But preserve me from the regular bargain humer ! Many lessons are learned during a bazaar—some worth remembering—others better forgotten. But whatever is lost, experience is gained, and that surely is worth something. A better way than raffling as a means of disposing of expensive articles still remains to be found. Those people who object to it on principle are at a disadvantage.. Many of the best things remain day after day, and at the end are sold for half- price or not at all, when they would otherwise have realized their full value, consequently swelled the takings and made the total receipts compare more favourably with those taken by the raffiers. But sacrifices have to be made for principle !

SOCIAL SERMONETTES FOR WORKING MEN.

BY JOHN TAYLOR

Author of "Search Lights on 14,r4, Labour and Social Problems."

===== 0002601_19041119_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
TRUSTS AND COMBINES.

"Oh, it is excellent, To ha.ve a giant's strength; but it is tyranßous To izse it like a giant.

—Shakespeare. I have received a long and interesting letter from one of my readers in the eastern counties about the growth of trusts and combines. The burden of his complaint is that "the trade and wealth of the country are getting more and more under the control of wealthy monopelies of this kind, resulting in high prices to the consumer, and oppression of the worker." And he is afraid that "the evil will spread in this country, as it has spread in America and Germany, till we have a few million- aires, and millions of slaves and paupers." My friend uses some very strong language in de- nouncing the capitalist manufacturers who have "pooled "their factoriesand workshops into trusts and combines. He calls them "brigands " and " thieves," and if he had his own way with them some or them would probably get a "short shrift and a long rope." He would hang them first and try them afterwards.

1.-TRUSTS AND COMBINES ARE NOT NECESSARILY EVIL.

Surely my friend will admit that employers of labour have just as much right to combine for their mutual protection as the vvorkpeople. Whether their combina- tions are wise, or prudent, or profitable, is another matter, but that is their look out. A trades unionist who joins an association of working men for tho benefit of labour, can hardly condemn a manufacturer who joins another trades union for'the benefit of capital. Such unions of capitalist employers have been forced on us by the altered conditions of most of our industries.

Half a century ' ago any workman who understood his trade, and could persuade somebody to guarantee him the loan of a few hundred pounds to start with, might begin manufacturing on his own account. I have known spinners, and weavers, and hatters, and shoemakers start with little or no capital, and after a few years of struggle and effort, they have built factories of their own and made their fortunes.

But they could not do it now if they had to start , again, because the circumstances and conditions of production have changed. Two things have happened in recent years to bar the poor man out of the race for weath. In the first place, hand labour and individual effort cannot compete against modern machinery, and some of our machines are so costly that only rich men can buy them. So it requires ten times as much capital toistart a factory now, with any prospect of success as it needed forty or fifty years ago. In the second place, the area of our markets for pro- duce has spread all over the world, and we are sending our products to the ends of the earth. No poor man can find money enough to buy all the raw materials he requires, and manufacture his goods, and hold them till his money comes back from abroad. Only rich men and wealthy syndicates, can find money enough to do these things successfully. These revolutionary- changes have given a rich producer an enormous advantage over a poor one, and they help to make a large, well- managed concern more profitable than a small one. The man who has plenty of money can build a factory or workshop on the most economical lines. He can stock it with costly machinery. He can buy his raw material at the lowest cash prices. And when he has manufac- tured his wares he can hold them till the state of the mirket enables him to sell them at, a profit. On the other hand, the man who is short of capital has to buy from hand to mouth, and take long credit, or borrow money for which interest must be paid. When he is in a tight place, and hard up for money, he must dump his goods on the market at sale prices, and often at a ruinous sacrifice. Of course, the consumer gets the benefit of these cheap, dumped goods, but there is a fall in prices after every such transaction on a lArge scale. and the rich manufacturers have to suffer for the small ones. Now so long as trusts and combines are used to strengthen the weak producer and promote economies in production and steady market prices, and secure constant employment for the work-people,nobody has any right to quarrel with them. But when they abandon these legitimate and desirable enterprises,and begin raiding the pockets of theconsumers. and corner- ing all the necessaries of life we are compelled in self- defence to turn and rend them. lI.—TRUSTS AND CONIBINES TRY TO CAPTURE

===== 0002601_19041119_art0128.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GIFTS OF PROVIDENCE.

Their highest ambition is to capture all the natural. sources of wealth,and create monopolies in land or miner- als or oil,or cotton,o r whegt,or some other product of the earth that everybody needs,and then they can do infinite mischief to the consumer. Perhaps the best illustration of their methods is to be found in the history of the Standard Oil Trust which was formed in 1882 to buy up all the oil wells in the United States, and to secure a prohibitive import duty on foreign oil. It raised an enormous capital, and paid fancy prices for these wells, and then it bought up all the old refineries and factories, so it obtained control of the raw material and the manufactured article. Then it went to those oil merchants and traders who bought their oil from foreign wells, and insisted that they should trade only with their trust. If these men became tied house dealers with them they allowed them to carry on their trade. If they refused their offer, they opened an oil shop close by their rival's store, and sold oil to his customers for less money than he could buy it, till they had ruined him. Then they raised their prices at their own sweet %rill, because they had obtained a monopoly. But they could not have done these things if oil had not been a gift of God, that no man can create, and that it is one of the necessaries of life to millions of people. Rockfeller, the millionaire, and his confederates are able to take toll on our oil lamps and stoves, because they have captured for themselves what Gad gave to us all. _ _

The true remedy for all these forms of robbery is to nationalise the land, and the mines, and the oil wells and the collieries, and the quarries, and drive out the private owner from all the natural wealth that is the gift of Providence. The State should bay these harpies out at a fair and honest valnation, and apply tho rents and revenue's to the rlduotion of,our rates aud taxes. 111.—TRUSrS AND COARTNES ARE OPPOSED

===== 0002601_19041119_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
TO FREE TRADE.

They live and flourish in Protectionist countries like Germ tny and the United States if they can create and maintain monopolies. If their branch of trade is free, and open to competition, they aro undone. Nearly twenty years ago a huge "Whiskey Trust" was formed in America to buy up all the distilleries, and send up the price of Uncle Sam's favourite beverage. Its pro- moters thought they could control the market and squeeze the consumer, as the oil trust had done. They did not see that anybody can make whiskey, but nobody can make oil. And they,did not realise that to raise prices is to stimulate production. They sent up the price of whiskey, I admit, but in doing so they tempted other men to begin distilling it and underselling them. So, in the expressive Yankee vernacular, the trust bust up, and the boys lost their dollars." But why did it become bankrupt? Because there was free trade in whiskey making. If there had been a ring fence of Protection round the trust it might have lived to this day. Take another illustration. In 1888 the Salt Union, with a capital of four millions, and the United Alkali Company, with a capital of eight and a half millions, formed a syndicate in this country with the aSowed object of creating a monopoly of the trade, and sending up prices. But their very snccess was their ruin. By sending up prices they tempted other men to bore for new brine wells, and build new alkali works, and undersell them in their own markets. So this over-capitalised concern was ruined by competition with the new producers that it raised up by inflated prices. The same causes usually produce the same results, everywhere, under like conditions and methods. If you buy factories and workshops at two or three times their actual value, and pay for them with watered stock on which you can never hope to pay any fair dividend you are violating the first principles of all honest trading, and leading your trusts and combines to the bankruptcy court. The Yorkshire Woolcombers' Association started with a great flourish of trumpets. It was going to do great things. It pooled 41 firms with a capital of nearly two millions. But it did not „•,lE3t a monopoly of the trade, for its rivals are able to compete with it, and defy it, so it has lost nearly all its capital, and a petition has been presented for winding it up. The Bleachers' Trust pooled fifty-three firms, with a capital of £6,750.009,but it failed to secure a monopoly of the trade. The Calico Printers pooled sixty firms, with a capital of £8,200,000, but they did not sweep all their rivals into their net, so they must stand the racket of over capitalization and watered stock and keen competition. The Fine Cotton Spinners and Doublers captured forty-seven firms, with a capital of £6,650,000, but they did not get A monopoly of the trade, and they have not been able to shut their rivals out of the market.

But I need not multiply examples of these colossal failures, you can find them everywhere. The only English combines that have had any measure tf success are the "Wall Paper Combine," and "J. and P. Coates Limited," and they are both monopolists. The Wall Paper Combine drew ninety-eight per cent of our manu- facturers into the net, and so long as it can keep them there and crush out all its opponents it may flourish. If any considerable number of firms broke away from it, and competed with it, the whole concern woult,l come to grief. J. and P. Coates had a great reputation for thread, and when they bought up and absorbed their four great rivals, they created a big monopoly, with It world-wide and well-earned popularity, that no rival can break down or destroy.

The working man usually fares badly under these monolS'Olies. The German Kartells "nob only try to regulate the conditions of sale, and to fix prices, and to limit the output, but they wage war against the trade unions, and seek to increase the supply of cheap labour. They arbitrarily raise prices to the consumer, and there- fore they increase the cost of living They crush out competitors by improper and unjust methods, and they encourage Protectionist monopolies in their most offen- sive and dangerous forms. Do you ask me what we can do to resist them? Cut their claws, and draw their teeth, by taking the land and minerals and railways and all the natural sources of wealth out of their hands. Then stimulate production that will compete with them. Our co-oeerative,societies are acting wisely when they build their own factories and workshops, and manufacture their own goods, and every new independent producer can drive another nail into the coffin of monopoly. I do not believe that these trusts and dombines, of which my correspondent is so much afraid, will spread in this country as they have spread in Germany and America. Our Free Trade soil and climate are un- favourable to their growth. They only flourish in Pro- tectionist countries. There are some evils that work out their own cure, and trusts and combinations will always do that in Free Trade countries. The American Shipping Trust that bought up the Leyland and the White Star steamers has , learned a thing or two lately. It is carrying passengers across the Atlantic for less than the cost of their keep, in order to spite and ruin its rivals, or force them into its pool. It will have to stop these games before long, for the simple reason that it cannot afford to do it for ever. When the shareholders have lost their capital and burnt their fingers they will adopt saner and wiser methods.

To me the most dangerous and perilous aspect of trusts and combines is their power to intimidate Gov- ernments and Parliaments. They can run candidates of their own at elections. They can threaten Cabinets and Ministries, and if every big trust could secure a bill of its own, like the Brewers' Endowment Bill,there would soon be an end of all our boasted fre3dom.

But we have nothing to fear in this country as long as we turn a deaf ear to the Birmingham Protectionists and Retaliators and stick to the principles of Free Trade and preserve an open door for the supply of all our needs. Free England can never be eaten up by trusts and combines, so long as she retains her Free Trade. She must sacrifice her freedom and become en- slaved before she can be robbed and plundered by the millionaires. And if she voluntarily parts with her freedom she will richly deserve all she gets and more. "He that hath ears to hear, let him hear."

THE WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY'S CONCERT.

The deplorable meagreness of the audiences at many of the Musical Society's concerts in recent seasons has, it must be confessed, become by reiteration a somewhat trite thing to remark. It is doubly regrettable, how- ever, that the interesting programme which opened the season on Tuesday evening should have been largely thrown away when regarded from the point of view of its educational and refining possibilities. Nor is disappoint- ment arising from the apparent futility of praiseworthy enterprise of this kind lessened by the reflection that the few who listened to the music performed are alone aware of the good things missed by the too numerous absentees. The local difficulties of interesting the multitude in the higher branches of music are yet far from being surmounted. Would that the society's management may in the near future haply hit upon some solution of the problem. The society's presentation of Goring Thomas's "Swan and Skylark" which formed the principal feature of the concert was, it is pleasing to record, both felicitous and worthy. It is little more than a decade since the composer,—then barely in the prime of life—met with a tragic death while yet the score was in process of completion. Yet the interval is sufficient to make clear the fact that English music has distinctly suffered from the premature withdrawal of one of its most lustrous contributors, in the beauty and charm of whose work are discerned the special gifts of a refined and delicate genius. The cantata is a startling admixture of sadness and joyousness, of tender pathos and ecstatic rapture, and there are those who claim to see in the note of , profound melancholy struck in the " Swan-song "—one of the most tender and betwitching of tenor solos—a premonition or foreshadowing on the composer's part of his apProaching lamentable end. Grounds for such a theory might certainly be found in the following words, allied to most expressive and moving music:

"But thou, sweet boon! too late Poured on my parting breath, vain gift of song Why cam'st thou thus, o'ermastering, rich and strong In the dark hour of fate Only to wake the sighs Of echoing-voices from the sparry cell ; Only to say-0 sunshine and blue skies! 0 life and love! farewell. And again in the setting of Mrs.liemans's touching lines addressed to Nature

"---thou who blendest thus The dirge-note and the song of festival !" But passing to that more cheerful section of the cantata in which exuberant joyousness is, with so striking an antithesis and with so artistic an effect, set against the gloom of the earlier portion, we have here, surely, a vindication of the composer's normal and happier mood. The contrast of the dying swan's lament and the sky- lark's blithesomeness, which may be described as strong without being forced, provides the composer with wide scope, the music, regarded as a whole, being seen to be graceful and prettily expressive rather than strongly passionate or impressively moving. That the musical society's members grasped the subtleties of this charming work was early apparent. There are some extremely complex and trying passages for the massed voices, often in intimate assocition with the vocal solos, many of which are rendered still more exacting by reason of the unusually high pitch to which the sopranos are often. led. In dealing with these and the many other difficulties of preserving a crisp attack and noting the ever-recurring changes in expression there were, on the part of.the rank and file of the chorus, few and negligible blemishes, while there was on the other hand, much that was worthy of all praise. Exquisite taste was shown in the choral support given to the solo tenor's farewell chant "Summer I depart." Especially fine too, was the. rendering of the highly I picturesque chorus commencing "Soft summer clouds were floating round him," while some massive effects were realised in the spirited and latterly much involved number "The summer is come, she h.ath said Rejoice!" all of which serves to show that the society has made a most satisfactory and propitious beginning of its season's work. Of the singing of the parts. for the principal voices we can speak in terms of high praise. Miss Mabel Henshall—one of the society's members who it will be remembered lately so signally distinguished herself in amateur light opera—sang the .single yet important contralto solo "Adieu! thy plaintive anthem fades" in a manner which added fresh laurels. to her name and dempnstrated at once both her versatility and her high artAtic and vocal abilities. The solo, conjoined as it is with a sweetly subdued chorus and the most refined orchestral colouring, has about it a fascinating sadness which Miss Henshall most fittingly expressed. Miss Lydia Nervil dealt with the soprano music,d9picting the skylark's rapturous ontbursts,with all the daintiness and brilliance which this exacting part demAnds. Possessing a voice of singular beauty and depth of tone, of great range and flexibility, it is scarcely necessary to say that the trills and bird-like roulades heard at the entry of the part were vocalised with consummate ease and grace,and that the telling solo immediately following was characterised by great and appropriate animation. Nor have we anything but compliments for Mr. James Davis and IVlr.James Coleman the tenor and bass principals. The former in rendering the impassioned swan-song and the latter in delivering the eloquent prologue and epilogue each contributed his quota of excellence in a present- ment of the cantata which, as has been already remarked, was entirely adequate and uniformly worthy. The rich instrumentation of the cantata, for which Dr. C. Villiers Stanford is responsible, was finely and ably interpreted by a full and representative orchestra, the very arduous duties of conducting which,and of guiding the choral vocalists through the intricacies of the score, devolved upon and were most capably discharged by Mr. Crossley.

In the second half of the programme there were some miscellaneous items of more than ordinary interest. In this category must be placed the two coupled orchestral pieces composed by Mr. Crossley, oat) of which—the Valse lente—was heard in public for the first time at this concert. These items by their melodic wealth and by the variety and effectiveness of their treatment clearly predicate on the part of their author a marked originality and a resourcefulnesss which in the fulness of time may reasonably be expected to eventuate in more ambitious efforts. The performance of both pieces was greeted with loud applause. Ths part-song "Lullaby of Life': (Leslie) proved a most enjoyable number, in the delights of which the society's members obviously revelled as much as did the audience. 'Not for a long time past has there been heard at these con- certs a inorceau of th? kind so sweetly and perfectly rendered. Miss Nervil's singing of the recit. and air from Meyerbeer's "Les. Huguenots" was an inspired performance, her splendid vocalisation of the brilliant concluding cadenza so rousing enthusiasm that a repetition of it could not be denied. Mr. Coleman would do better to dispense with ad lib. string or other parts in such songs as "Till death " (Mascheroni) the ineffectiveness of such accompaniments being here clearly noticeable as a distinct distraction in realising the full effect of an otherwise fine song. An emphatic recall was however given to Mr. Coleman, response being made with Tosti's " Speak !" The sing- of "0 vision entrancing " from Goring Thomas's " Esmeralda " by Mr. James Davis, was both con- scientious and artistic, and such was his success that Mr. Davis was constrained to grant a further favour in the shape of a charming rendering of "I'll sing thee songs of Araby," which, of course, still further delighted the audience. In memory of the late Dr. Hiles's long and honour- able connection with the society an impressive perform- ance of Chopin's Funeral March was given as a prelude to entering upon the evening's programme.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

(By " Ovalist.",

Like many other loyal Lancastrians from Warrington I hied myself to Oldham. on Saturday- to witness the annual Battle of the Roses. The Cheshire Lines Com- mittee very conveniently ran a cheap excursion and it was well patronised. To a great extent county matches under the Northern Union are on exhibition lines, but there is sufficient earnestness about the pro- ceedings when Lancashire, Yorkshire, or Cheshire meet to satisfy the most warlike spectator. Saturday's engagement was specially interesting for on it depended Lancashire's last chance of the Championship which they have held for so many seasons. Yorkshire had a powerful side, and it was generally thought that the mixed forces of Lancashire would have to excel them- selves in order to win. Warringtonians had a keen interest in the encounter, for were not Fish, Preston, and Elockenhall dporting the Lancashire jersey? It was the latter's debut in a county match and hope was general that he would prove an ideal partner for the redoubtable Lomas at half-back.

Lancashire started off in a style which boded well for the final result. The forwards proved smart at getting possession, and the backs soon proved them- selves, a more aggressive combination than that opposed to them. Yorkshire were soon driven back, Lomas and Fish being very prominent in the opera- tion. The " irrepressible " was particularly spry. and one beautiful bit of dodgery in front of the main sthrid was cheered to the echo. A moment later War- rington's idol was laid low. He nipped in cleverly but before he could get going a Yorkshire forward downed him heavily, restoratives and massage being required. Revenge soon came in the shape of a penalty, which Fish converted into a beautiful goal. This success was well earned by Lancashire,who looked like trouncing the Tykes handsomely. Fish was soon to the fore again with an effort that eclipsed everything else. Yorkshire were passing the ball in the Lanca- shire half when the wily one snapped up Riley's transfer and a shout arose as he came clean away at full speed. Would he pass the full back ? Well, rather ! He sailed all round Gunn and scored behind the posts in magnificent fashion, only to fail at the goal-kick through carelessness. The cheering was loud and continuous nevertheless.

Then the tide turned and Fortune turned her back on the Red Rose. Hockenhall who had been playing an effective game, was to the fore in a dashing dribble over the Yorkshire line. He dived for the ball and appeared to score, but all he got was a nasty gash on the head from somebody's ill-directed boot, and poor Tommy was carried off unconscious to take no further part in the game. From this point Lanca- shire were doomed. The removal of a big man like Ferguson from the forwards placed them at the mercy of the Yorkshire eight and also disorganised the Lancashire back division. The White Rose began to bloom, while the red em- blem of Lancashire drooped lamentably. Early in the second half Yorkshire took the lead with a couple of tries by Wattie Davies, and Lancashire never got a chance of handling the ball. Further points were put on in quick style anc: at the close Yorkshire, who played a great game when the fates favoured them, won by 14 points to 5 points.

Hockenhall's injury was the turning point of the game, and I never remember seeing the loss of one man make such a difference to a side. Lancashire were simply overwhelmed fore and aft, but I am convinced that they would have won had they re- mained at full strength. They were so manifestly superior' that they appeared able to outplay the Yorkshire men in every department, and further points must have accrued. Probably Yorkshire would have scored before the close, but I think they would have been well in the rear when hostilities ceased. Casualties are the fortune of war, and what was Lancashire's and Flockenhall's misfor- tune was Yorkshire's gain. The visitors certainly took full advantage of their opportunities and played fine football in the second half.

The heroes of the Lancashire side were Fish, Lomas, and Poole, while the one utter failure was Hogg, Rumour hath it that he was not well. In charity I hope so. He gave a pitiable exhibition of slowness and hesitancy. Speaking of Fish, the "Manchester Guardian" says: "He is a fearless fellow this Fish, and well named, for he has all the resource and almost the speed of movement of a hunted trout making for 'vantage pools in a brook. And he is hard as nails. Snapping up the ball once he shot away with it, but his movement had been anticipated, and a Yorkshire three-quarter, already in his stride, bounded after the War- rington man before he had got his stride and brought him down heavily. Fish's comrades lifted him up and signalled for the man of sponge and towel. For a minute or two Lancashire people were aenninely anxious. It looked rather worse than a winding, but Fish was soon himself again, and was well enough to kick a goal when the Yorkshire forwards got pena- lised for a wrong formation in the scrum. Very soon afterwards he smartly intercepted a pass between Yorkshire three-quarters, catching it in its flight but not surely so that it was almost a knock-on, and then bolted for the line. There was only the full back in the way, and Fish went past him easily as if he were merely a dummy figure needing only to be avoided."

Marsden was the one outstanding figure on the Yorkshire side, and his display was really magni- ficent. I don't think there can be any doubt that he is the cleverest and most aggressive half in the Union and the Bradford team are to be congratulated on possessing such a player. The openings he made were such as any back could score from, and every point but one which Yorkshire recorded was the out- come of Marsden's individual excellence. The other men supported him well and combined cleverly when Lancashire had lost control of the game. Gunn was unreliable at full back and compared unfavourably with Poole, who gave an admirable display.

The championskip should now be a site thing for Yorkshire, unless the persevering Cestrians put up another surprise. They played Lancashire a surpris- ing game, and will give Yorkshire a busy afternoon.

Meanwhile, poor Warrington, deprived of the ser- vices of Fish, Hockenlaall, Preston and Boardman, were catching it hot on the banks of the Humber. Hull Kingston Rovers played a great game, and made Warrington look a decidedly inferior crowd. Chambers at half back startled Warrington by the way he kept bursting through, and the heaviness of the score 14-0, was de to his efforts. Brooks and Short made only poor substitutes, and it would have been probably better policy to have played the two Davies's at half and Brollies on the wing. However the result would have been the same, and Warrington. must just bide their time till the Rovers appear at Wilderspool.

Bradford were in great danger of losing two points, seeing that they had six men playing for the County but the reserves gave such an excellent account of themselves that Runcwn were defeated on their own ground by 11-0. This puts Bradford on top, and I rather fancy they will be there at the end of the season.

There has been a gratifying increase in the number of Junior Rugby teams in the district this season. Some then should provide players of promise for seniors service in course of time. I notice that in the match between Padgate Juniors and Legh-street on Satur- day, a player named Furness scored the whole of the l 8 points, which were the total of the Padgate youths' superiority. This is lively even among Juniors.

It is just a month since Warrington performed at Wilderspool, on which occasion they beat Wigan. There should be a warm welcome to Hallam and Co., to-day, therefore. Batley furnish the opposition and this should be one of the easy matches of the season. The "gallant youths" have deteriorated and at present, seem more likely to become possessors of the wooden spoor than the Northern Union Cup.

Warrington will be able to put a good team in the field, though Hockenhall and Boardman are still on the injured list. Preston will resume, and J. Davies is being given a chance in place of Hockenhall. The experiment of playing the two Davies's together will be watched with interest. If "A. " team form is any thing to go by, the Penygraig man should please the critics.

Hockey. [BY R.G.P.]

Last week the Stockton Heath first eleven had an open date, but the second team were playing Sale Moor at Grappenhall-road, and managed to defeat the visitors by no loss than 7-0. Sale Moor played three backs and no goalkeeper, which cost them several goals, and seeing that the back division is the weakest part of their teatu this seems a funny way of trying to win matches.

The home team played well, especially the forwards who seem to have cultivated a nice passing game. The usual weak spot, inside right, was filled by W. Richard- son, who had quite a day out, and finished by obtaining the last three goals, the others being scored by W. Longdin(3),and D. Skelton(l).qL.Robertson at half back was somewhat lost as to his right position, but when he gets settled down, he has the makings of a good half.

I am now in a position to say, that the Stockton Heath club will, next year, play on thr, Warrington Cricket ground. This will be one or tlia best grounds in the north, and it is hoped the club will be favoured with some of the county fixtures.

This afternoon the Ist team go to Eccles, and from paper form should score an easy victory, while the• second who will travel to Sale Moor with a depleted team, may meet more than their match.

Ist v.Eccles away; R. G. Pierpoint, goal; L. Fair- dough and E. Pierpoint, backs; E. E. Woods, Eric Fairclough and J. Richardson, halves; C. H. Bond, S. Baxter R. Pierpoint, W. Richardson and R.,Fairclough, forwards : Train leaves Central Station 1.55. Referee Mr. Bellamy.

Cross-Country Running.

Warrington Harriers had an inner-club run with Win- nington Harriers on Saturday in which they were assist- ed by A. Shrubb, the most renowned runner of the day, and it was no doubt owing to his presence that so large a crowd assembled to witness the start. The home team mustered forty runners. whilst Winnington only turnei out seven athletes. The issue was decided on the first men to arrive home, and when the totals came to be reckoned it was found that Warrington had won easily, five individuals claiming leading positions. so that the Cestrians were outnumbered by 82 The - The trail was laid over q- miles of what is known as the championship course, a most difficult country, and one sufficient to test the ibilities of even the most matured runner. The trail was laid by four of the home club and almost from the inception of the con- test proper, Shrubb took the lead,which was increased at every point. He finally won by a quarter of a mile in 38 minutes. Straw the Warrington champion, came- in second a minute and twenty seconds behind. The teams finished in the following order :—A. Shrubb, 1;. 1, C. J. Straw; 2, S. D. Parsonage; 3, J. Parsonage, 4; W. C. Brereton, 5; total 15 points. Winnington, Barnton, 8 ; Albeston, 11 ; Prayne, 21 ; Bennett, 25; Hatton, 32 ; total 97 points. Before the start the War- rington pack assembled on the cricket field, where they were photographed by Mr. Birtles. Shrubb has promised to assist Warrington again before the season closes.

EDUCATION IN WARRINGTON. Some Examination Results.

The Education Committee have this week published' in detailed form the results of the examinations held early this year in science, art and commercial subjects. Comparisons are drawn with the result of similar examinations conducted last year,and in the total in each of the three sections a gratifying increase in the per— centage of successes gained by the students, is recorded. To particularise: In the science section the number of candidates presented showed a. decrease of 20 as compared with last year, whereas the number of passes were only nine fewer. In the art section there was a decrease of over 100 in the number of students examined with a decrease in the number of passes of three. In the commercial classes the number examined was 246 as against 335 in 1903, whereas the number of passes was 204 against 266. The examina- tions under the auspices of the "City and Guilds of London Institute, and the worshipful company of increase in are also given and show an all-round in regard to the number examined and the percentag e of passes. The successes gained in the evening continuation schools taken collectively show, a percentage of 79. Taken separately. Fairfield school heads the- list with 38 passes out of 42 'girls examined, though the subjects taken were of an almost purely domestic char- acter. In the Warrington British schools (People's College), with the usual evening school curiculum 120 scholars out of 140 satisfied the examiners,whilst in the - other l three schools Heathside (boys), Warrington Parochial (boys), and Wycliffe British (girls), the per- centage of success was fairly equal. being respectively 63, 65 and 64.

Taken on the whole the results are highly pleasing, and must be a source of satisfaction to the Education Committee.

AWONDERFUL DISCOVERY.—This is the age of research and experiment, when all, nature, so to speak. is ransacked by the scientific for the comfort and happiness of man. The Examiner, Saturday, November 195 1904 FOOTBALL. • HOCKEY.—If one firm of manufacturers more than another keeps abreast of the times in the matter of Implements for Sport, that firm 18 GEO- G. BUSSE & Co. of London, who are leading the way again with their Hockey Clubs and Balls which bear th.ir well-known trade mark. Ask the local Sport,. Dealer for G.G.B. & Co.'. Special Hockey List. If not procurable apply to 36 & 38. Queen Victoria at., London. Factory—Pezkbam, S.B. (64). FRIENDLY MATCH. WARRINGTON " A" V SALFORD ST. BARTHOLOMEWS. —The home team secured another easy victory as the result of this match, which was played at Wilderspool on Saturday. The opening stages were in Warrington's favour, but later the game was mare evenly contested. The visitors • made several bold attempts to get over, but the Warrington tackling was sound. The home team played up vigorously, and Davies after a good run passed to Roberts who dashed over. Knight added a goal. Before the interval A. Litton added another try somewhat easily, the goal kick being unsuccessful. Half-time: Warrington, 3 points; Salford, nil. The game had not long been re-started when Khight landed a penalty goal. Soon afterwards Davies scored an uncon- verted try near the corner. Jordan got nicely away, and giving to Barnes, the latter further augmented the score, Knight improving. Through faulty play by the War- rington backs one of the visitors rushed over, and the goal kick being successful, the final score read :7-War- rington, 18 points; Salford St. Bartholomew s, five points.

WAK-BRINGTON BOARD OF ,lUARDIANS. Re Mr. Jolley aid " :he Clannar of Selfish Ignorah, -sidency This Board met on Wednesday under the pr. qrs• re Mr. A. Brooks. Ther were also present •r Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson,Rev. T. R. Matthews, Rev. ?aro. Mercer, Dr. Murray, Dr. Patterson, Messrs.A. W. Hickman, T. H. Ashcroft, S. Davies, G. Thompson, R. Forshaw, J. Backhouse, R. Bolton, J. Tinton, W. Flannagan. M. W. Seton, J. Ball, G. Mather, J. C Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright, (superintendent of Padgate Schools). CHRISTMAS TREAT FOR PADGATE CHILDREN. At a meeting of the Padgate Schools Committee it was decided that the usual Christmas treat be given to the children, also that £7 10s. be allowed for the pur- pose of prizes to be awarded as in previous years. Mr. MATER moved the adoption of the minutes. Mr. ASHCROFT seconded and the motion was carried. PERAMBULATORS WANTED. THE GIFT OF A PIANO TO THE INMATES. At a meeting of the House Committee it was stated that perambulators in which to take out young children would be a great boon, and the hope was expressed that by giving publicity to these requirements, the Guardians might receive offers of the same. Mrs. Clay announced that the Co-operative Society had decided to present to the Guardians a piano for the use of the inmates, and the committee in accepting the generous gift directed that the Master should convey to the society the warm thanks of the committee as representing the hoard. The instrument will be formally handed over this (Satur- day) evening. Mr. MATHER asked whether Mr. Hickman could not provide some perambulators. ' Mr. HICKMAN: Yes, I will get one to carry you and me. (Laughter.) Mr. JOLLEY said the Co-operative Society had made a very handsome gift which called for special recogni- tion. He was informed that the piano was a very good one, and the thanks of the Board were certainly due to the Society for providing amusement for the inmates which, he had no doubt, would he of very great benefit. He moved that a special vote of thanks be accorded to the Society. Mrs. WILKINSON seconded. , Mr.IMATHER endorsed all that Mr. Jolley had said. He rather envied the Workhouse at having such kind friends, and he would be very pleased to have friends like them for the Cottage Homes at Padgate. Mr. THOMPSON : I think the Chairman of the Pad- gate Schools Committee might present a piano to the Homes. I suggest that a vote of thanks be accorded to him. (Laughter.) The CHAIRMAN : Or else an order for the house. (Laughter). The motion was agreed to and the minutes were con- firmed. MORE ABOUT THE BUILDING EXTENSION. MR. JOLLEY'S STRONG COMMENTS. The Clerk said he had already received a number of letters on the subject of the probable loan the Board would require in respect of the new building, but they might stand over for the Board's considemtion.He did not suppose they would improve upon the Government rate. Later the CLERK read the following letter from the Warrington Property-Owners' Association :—Dear Sir, —I am instructed to forward you a copy of resolutions unanimously passed at a meeting of the above associa- tion on Monday evening last and shall be glad if you will kindly bring same before the next meeting of Guardians. "That this association representing a rate- able value of upwards of £120,000 emphatically protests against any further expenditure for new buildings in connection with the Warrington Workhouse. Consider- ing the present depressed state of trade and the prospective increase in the rates, they are of opinion that it is an inopportune time for any such expenditure and trust that the Board of Guardians will reconsider their decision to erect new buildings at a cost of £7,000." They also consider that all building schemes for the present ought to be suspended, unless absolutely necessarv.—Yours truly. T. J. Hoeruss, Secretary. Mr. I-Irciof&N said that in view of the letter he would move that all new building schemes in that Union besuspended for twelve months. He would point out that a number of corporate bodies were endeavour- ing to do all they possibly could to defer new schemes in consequence of the state of trade and the heavy burden of the rates. If the Board deferred the present extension for twelve months they would be in good company in the course they adopted. The CHAIRMAN: This is practically the same resolu- tion you proposed a few meetings ago. At the present time we are considering the letter of the Property Owners' Association and the question is shall we answer it. and if so how. Mr. HICKMAN: The resolution is a little bit differ- ent because it applies to all new building schemes. Of course, if you rule me out of order— -- The CHAIRMAN : I don't wish to rule you out of order. Mr. HICKMAN: I think when I have finished the Board will perhaps see some reason why they should accept my resolution and reply to the letter. Mr. JOLLEY :I submit as a matter of order that this is another attempt to rescind a resolution without notice, and it is wrong. Mr. DAVIES asked for the Clerk's :tiling as to whether the motion was out of order. The CLERK said the Board had finally got through all the preliminaries. He would however receive forms of resolution and give a month's notice to each Guardian before they could finally consent to the building and to the borrowing of tho money for the purpose. The subject would keep moving on slowly until it reached that stage. He thought it would be competent for the Board to stop the proceedings if they were so disposed or to adjourn them, subject to the remark already made whichlought to influence them to some extent, that the Board had practically already decided that the building should be proceeded with. Mr. HicitaiaN: Do I understand that I cannot move the resolution? The Civanmax : I don't say you are out of order. If the Board wish to hear you lam quite willing. You are moving practically the same resolution as before, only it is differently worded. Mr. ASHCROFT: Cannot Mr. Hickman give notice of the resolution and discuss it at the next meeting? The CHAIRMAN : At the present time we are sup- posed to be discussing the letter. Are we to reply to it or are we not, and if so in what terms? Mr. FORSHAW moved that the letter lie on the table. The people who had written the letter were not in possession of the whole of the facts. They did not understand the matter as the members of the Board did. The Guardians were elected as representatives of the ratepayers and surely they were able to manage the business of the Workhouse. If not, they had better vacate their seats and let other people come and manage it. The CHAIRMAN : Wouldn't it be better to acknow-. ledge the letter? Don't let us treat them too cavalierly Some little time ago when the Trades Council wrote* asking to he allowed to do some of our work we acknow- ledged the letter. Mr. FORSHAW : I move that the letter be acknow- ledged. The Rev. Father MERCER seeonded. Mr. BA.CKHOUSE : What proposition of the rateable value of Warrington does the Association represent? The CHAIRMAN: They say they represent £120,000., The Rev. Father MERCER: According to their own' statement, they represent a quarter of the full assess- able value of the Union and about half of Warrington. Mr. ASHCROFT said the Association must not be ignored. He advised the Board to acknowledge the letter in a suitable manner. The Association was a power.in Warrington, whereas the country Guardians had nothing to do with the town. Mr. MATHER : They have to pay the rates of the Union. Mr. JOLLEY: lam entirely with Mr. Ashcroft. I think the letter ought to be acknowledged in a suitable , manner. I would like to propose that the Clerk be ' instructed to acknowledge the receipt of the letter and inform these people that in the opinion of the Guardians it is to be regretted that persons boasting the posses- sion of so much wealth—who claim to represent a quarter of the wealth of the Union—should be so deficient in intelligence and humanity. He should also be instructed to tell them that we propose as Guardians of the poor to discharge our duties to the sick, the poor, and the aged without any attention to the clamour of selfish ignorance from outside. (Cries of "No, no.") Mr.'ASHCROFT : I hope the reporters will put that down. Mr. SETON said he would like the Board to know that Earlestown people were complaining of the deci- sion to extend the building. He thought the Board might give a little more out-relief instead of providing more accommodation. He would like the letter to be answered in a proper minner. The Earlestovvn people contemplated calling an indignation meeting. Mr. HICKMAN said he hoped Mr. Jolley's rider would not be put in the letter. He hoped they as members of the Board would not attempt to insult the ratepayers by replying in that strain. He thought the ratepayers ought to receive proper treatmentand due consideration He did not think the Board ought to fling a reply like that Mr. Jolley suggested in the teeth of any rate- payers. The Rev. T. R. MATTHEWS : May I suggest that we send a polite acknowledgment of the letter. Mr. HICKMAN: That is all they expect. They let you know by the letter what they think. If you acknowledge it you will be doing an act of courtesy to them. Mr. JOLLEY said that from the emphasis with which Mr. Matthews, whom they all respected, used the adjective polite, he appeared to suggest that what he (Mr. Jolley) said was not polite. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Thompson said • hear, hear. He (Mr. Jolley) was very strongly of the opinion that it was perfectly polite but whether there was any difference on that point, what he said was absolutely true. There could be no difference of opinion about that. The people who had written the letter, as Mr. Forshaw properly said, had written it in ignorance. He was not in the least afraid of saying
